Abstract
The results of the project “Testing AI as a Rational Theologian” carried out 
from 2023 to 2024 show that leading chatbots powered by large language 
models, such as ChatGPT based on GPT-4o and Gemini based on Gemini 
1.5, possess the core skills of a rational theologian. Testing, based on Chris­
tian theological materials, has revealed that these chatbots accurately in­
terpret biblical passages—providing context, conveying the correct mean­
ing (including according to the theory of the four senses of Scripture), and 
solving logical puzzles related to difficult passages. The chatbots can also 
clarify theological problems, critically assess theological reasoning and 
formulate such reasoning, as well as logically link biblical utterances to 
theological statements. The chatbots are capable of discussing natural the­
ology and can provide theological guidance. Finally, they have proven that 
they can apply the concept of a rational theologian by creating their own 
criteria for such a figure, and that they are able to evaluate the theological 
skills of other language models. Some limitations have been detected, such 
as hallucination when quoting theological sources and mistakes in logi­
cal inference. Moreover, the chatbots gave different answers when asked a 
practical question within the framework of the Islamic tradition, accord­
ing to the Hanafi school. A simplified benchmark for theological skills 
assessment enabled us to measure their performance: ChatGPT achieved 
an overall score of 86%, while Gemini scored 84%. This report presents 
the study’s methodology and tools (tasks and assessment criteria), which 
can also be used to evaluate other chatbots. It includes a discussion of the 
results and an assessment of the theological skills of both chatbots. An ap­
pendix contains the full transcripts of conversations with ChatGPT and 
Gemini analysed in the study.
Keywords: chatbots, large language models, artificial intelligence, 
theology, religion, rationality, benchmarks, testing, performance

--- Page 8 ---

8
Executive Summary
A general research question of this project was: Can artificial intelligence 
(AI) function as a rational theologian? It was assumed that we could test 
chatbots based on large language models (LLMs) to check whether they 
had the skills, knowledge, and credibility necessary to provide high-quali­
ty responses in theological conversations, and whether they could serve as 
rational interlocutors in such discussions.
In this context, the term “rational” has at least two components: (1) the 
negative one, understood as avoiding irrational thinking, sometimes called 
anti-irrationalism, and (2) the positive one, namely: using logic and philo­
sophical concepts to discuss theological issues, solve theological problems, 
formulate philosophical argumentation, and analyse theological reasoning.
The initial stage of the project started in September 2022, just before the 
launch of ChatGPT based on GPT-3, which was a milestone in the develop­
ment of LLMs. Pilot testing was conducted in 2023, and included: ChatGPT, 
powered by GPT-3.5, Bard powered by PaLM, Bing Chat (later renamed 
Copilot) powered by GPT-4, and Llama2. This phase of the project enabled 
us to refine the testing scenario and was complemented by additional testing. 
The final stage of formal testing was conducted in 2024 and included two 
selected chatbots: ChatGPT and Gemini, powered by (respectively): GPT-4o 
(in some cases compared with GPT-o1-mini) and Gemini 1.5.
The investigation concentrated on the following four research ques­
tions. Key findings from the study are presented beneath each question.
RQ1: How to measure AI competence in theology?
We can identify theological skills and define them through assessment cri­
teria which show the necessary actions to demonstrate each skill. Next, 

--- Page 9 ---

9
Executive Summary 
we design tasks which should be performed to meet such criteria. Since 
different criteria may refer to different levels of advancement with respect 
to those skills, the tasks are also structured to measure varying degrees 
of advancement. We developed a simple methodology and framework to 
evaluate the proficiency of LLM-powered chatbots in these selected skills, 
in order to determine whether they can serve as rational theologians.
RQ2: Are the existing models sufficiently trained to function as 
rational theologians and to solve theological problems?
Yes, they are. They have both sufficient knowledge and skills to do that.
Knowledge is always “memorised” in the process of training. The testing 
shows that the dataset used to train the models included many philosophi­
cal and theological materials.
Some chatbots additionally use external sources, which are available on 
the internet. For instance, Copilot refers to websites and provides hyper­
links to these materials. The current version of ChatGPT also uses external 
sources.
Testing has shown that chatbots have many high-order skills, which en­
able them to solve theological problems and speak as rational theologians. 
They correctly interpret biblical passages. They can clarify theological 
problems. They critically assess theological reasoning and can formulate 
such reasoning, as well as logically link biblical utterances to theological 
statements. They can discuss natural theology, but also provide theologi-
cal guidance. Finally, they have proven that they can apply the concept of 
a rational theologian by creating their own criteria for such a figure.
RQ3: What valuable insights can AI offer regarding the rationality 
of religion?
LLM-powered chatbots generally avoid direct answers which evaluate the 
rationality of religion: whether religion as such, or any specific religion. 

--- Page 10 ---

10
Executive Summary 
They are designed to refrain from generating statements which could be in­
terpreted as personal beliefs, and instead aim to present a range of opinions.
However, when asked to analyse religious texts, they point out the uni­
versal truths they convey, and when presented with theological arguments, 
they do emphasise their strengths. They also provide users with rational 
argumentation for religious beliefs, although they also include counter­
arguments in order to show a complete picture of the debate.
Chatbots provide us with valuable analyses of biblical texts: They can 
go beyond the literal sense, and they explore many layers of interpretation. 
They also show that such texts can help us in many ways by teaching about 
the good life, as well as offering spiritual guidance. Thus, the chatbots indi­
rectly show that religion is not irrational. Quite the opposite: They can pro­
duce content that highlights that there are good reasons to follow a religion.
RQ4: Do the existing models need additional (theological) 
training?
Yes and no. They require no additional training to provide us with satisfac­
tory answers concerning theological problems and to give feedback about 
the rationality of religion. However, if we want them to perform special­
ised tasks in a particular manner, we should train them to ensure they 
can follow the desired pattern. Bruno Banelli and Ines Skelac proved that 
Llama-3 trained on the corpus of Pope Benedict XVI’s texts can better as­
sess theological argumentation.
Another useful method is retrieval augmented generation (RAG), 
which enables models to use external resources, and add the selected infor­
mation to prompts, in order to generate answers based on the knowledge 
from those sources.
The testing indicates that chatbots still require improvements if we 
want them to deliver correct logical reasoning and evaluations. While they 
exhibit advanced logical skills, they occasionally make mistakes in their 
own theological inferences and in assessing the reasoning of others.

1. Introduction
Can artificial intelligence (AI) serve as a theologian, capable of giving rea­
sonable answers to difficult questions concerning God and religion, and 
rationally analyse problems and arguments related to such topics? And if 
so, what kind of support can we expect from AI in this respect?
The main aim of the project “Testing AI as a Rational Theologian” was 
to develop tools to assess when AI would be ready for rational and substan­
tive discussions on theological problems and argumentation. The under­
lying assumption was that if chatbots powered by large language models 
(LLMs) are ready to perform such tasks, they could contribute meaning­
fully to the development of the human relationship with God and the 
world. AI could, thus, solve different problems and remove barriers to that 
relationship, using rational argumentation based on reliable knowledge.
This approach assumes that theological knowledge alone is insufficient 
when evaluating AI as a theologian. Even more important are its theologi­
cal, logical, and hermeneutical skills. Additionally, it is essential to check 
whether AI chatbots can reliably use sources (such as sacred scriptures and 
theological texts), and whether we can say that they are sincere when they 
provide us with that kind of support.
The idea of the project appeared when the development of language 
models made them capable of effectively communicating with humans. 
Since the famous 2019 debate with Project Debater, we have known that 
AI can formulate arguments, understand the arguments of the interlocu­
tor, and respond to them (Slonim et al., 2021). In the meantime, various 
applications of neural networks were tested also in theological context, 
including a successful attempt to build entity graphs of biblical names 
based on the Bible in different languages (Martinjak et al., 2023). In the late 
2010s, advances in hardware and the training of various language models, 
such as BERT, ERNIE, and later GPT-3 (Brown et al., 2020) and Switch 

--- Page 12 ---

12
1. Introduction 
Transformers (Fedus et al., 2022), made it clear that we were closer to the 
point at which AI would be ready to understand and solve very difficult 
problems, including the profound questions of philosophy and theology. 
This progress suggested the possibility that AI could provide instructive 
feedback concerning metaphysical theories, arguments for the existence 
of God or the status of sacred texts. Two questions arose: When might 
such a moment arrive, and how could we determine that such a moment 
had come? The address these questions, we developed a project centred on 
assessing chatbots’ theological skills through testing of selected models.
The preparatory stage of the project included desk research and a test 
of a simple chatbot based on AI, called ChAI, which showed its limited 
abilities with respect to theological discourse. This chatbot is no longer 
publicly available. The next step was the preliminary testing of ChatGPT, 
powered by GPT-3.5, released by OpenAI in November 2022. This evalua­
tion showed that such chatbots could meet at least the basic criteria for an 
automated theologian.
After the conception of the study had been prepared, along with the as­
sessment framework and preliminary benchmarks and tasks for measuring 
theological skills development, the pilot testing was performed. In August 
and September 2023, three leading chatbots were tested using the same 
scenario: ChatGPT (launched by OpenAI in November 2022, powered by 
GPT-3.5), Bing Chat (launched by Microsoft in February 2023, powered 
by GPT-4) and Bard (launched by Google in March 2023 with limited ac­
cess in the United States and the United Kingdom, and after a few months 
released in Europe, powered by PaLM). The results of this phase of testing 
were broadly discussed at several seminars and international conferences. 
The feedback received from many scholars, experts, and students helped 
to refine the methodology of the study and particular tasks. In the mean­
time, a chatbot based on the Llama2–70B model was also tested using the 
same tasks. In consequence, the final scenario, consisting of nine groups of 
tasks, was prepared. In the last stage of the project, during summer 2024, 
we tested two leading chatbots with superior performance capabilities: 
Gemini (launched by Google in December 2023, powered by Gemini 1.5) 

--- Page 13 ---

13
1. Introduction 
and ChatGPT (powered by GPT-4o, partially including GPT-o1-mini 
launched in September 2024).
Both the results of preliminary testing conducted in 2023 and the final 
evaluation of 2024 show, most notably, the surprisingly high performance 
of all examined chatbots with respect to theological skills. The models also 
provide reliable knowledge, although with some limitations and the risk of 
hallucination, especially when generating specific quotations. Neverthe­
less, these systems are continuously being improved to either provide more 
reliable content or acknowledge their limitations. As will be demonstrated 
below, the answer to the main research question of this report is positive.
LLMs and chatbots are constantly being refined. Many months have 
passed since the research was conducted, so in the meantime, new versions 
of the models and the chatbots which had been tested in this study have ap­
peared. However, the methodology used here is universal, as are the tasks 
presented below. They can be successfully used to test various chatbots. 
The conclusions presented here also appear to be largely universal.

2. Methodology
2.1. Aim and Research Questions
The aim of the project is to check whether selected chatbots can function 
as rational theologians. Additionally, four research questions have been 
defined to help fulfil the central aim:
•	 RQ1: How to measure AI competence in theology?
•	 RQ2: Are the existing models sufficiently trained to function as ra­
tional theologians and to solve theological problems?
•	 RQ3: What valuable insights can AI offer regarding the rationality 
of religion?
•	 RQ4: Do the existing models need additional (theological) training?
2.2. General Assumptions
Regarding the aim of the project, it should first be determined what “ra­
tional” means in this context. For the purposes of this study, “rational” 
comprises two components: (1) the negative one, understood as avoiding 
irrational thinking, sometimes called anti-irrationalism, and (2) the posi­
tive one, namely: using logic and philosophical concepts to discuss theo­
logical issues, solve theological problems, formulate philosophical argu­
mentation, and analyse theological reasoning.
A caveat is necessary concerning expressions such as “thinking” and 
“understanding.” We do not determine here whether models or chatbots 
genuinely have the ability to think or understand. We remain open to both 
Alan Turing’s (1950) views and the objections of John Searle (1980) based 

--- Page 15 ---

15
2.2. General Assumptions
on the Chinese room thought experiment. In this study, we employ these 
expressions to describe the operational aspects of chatbots’ activity, with­
out determining the nature of such actions.
Second, it is crucial to define what constitutes a “rational theologian.” The 
methodological assumption of this study is that this can be accomplished 
by enumerating the key skills of someone deserving of this designation.
In consequence, we also assume that such skills can be measured, 
which relates to RQ1. To enable this endeavour, we employ a method of de­
termining assessment criteria for each skill. Such criteria should precisely 
indicate what must be performed to establish that someone possesses the 
skill in question. They should be formulated using operational verbs, such 
as “analyses,” “indicates,” and “distinguishes,” to clearly determine the re­
quired actions.
Next, these criteria can be utilised to measure the proficiency level of a 
given skill. Various methods are available for this purpose. We can deter­
mine levels through the complexity of the operations to which the criteria 
refer. This method is employed in life-long learning tools, such as national 
qualification frameworks and standards of education and qualifications 
descriptions. The characteristics of several levels in qualification frame­
works (such as the European Qualification Framework with its eight levels, 
where level 1 represents basic skills and level 7 corresponds to a master’s 
degree) and the characteristics of skills determined in the standards of 
education or in descriptions of qualifications, are formulated to clearly in­
dicate the complexity of tasks to be performed, the level of independence, 
as well as the creativity and self-governance necessary for such tasks. To il­
lustrate, in the Polish Qualifications Framework, level 1 is characterised as 
follows: “able to carry out very simple tasks according to detailed instruc­
tions under typical conditions,” whereas level 6 is described as “able to 
innovatively complete tasks and resolve problems which are complex and 
non-routine under variable and not fully predictable conditions” (Ziewiec 
et al., 2016). This approach is to some extent inspired by Bloom’s taxono­
my (Bloom et al., 1956), which distinguishes six categories of educational 
goals, progressing from lower-order to higher-order skills:

--- Page 16 ---

16
2. Methodology 
•	 remembering—recall specific facts;
•	 understanding—grasp meaning of instructional materials;
•	 applying—use information in a new (but similar) situation;
•	 analysing—take apart the known and identify relationships;
•	 evaluating—examine information and make judgments;
•	 creating—use information to create something new.
According to these ideas, we can create assessment criteria which refer 
to different degrees of complexity, independence, creativity, etc. Hence, if a 
chatbot meets only basic criteria, we can assume that it possesses a certain 
competency at a fundamental level. However, we would classify it as hav­
ing advanced-level competency if it meets other criteria that impose higher 
requirements.
Alternatively, we can adopt a “flat” approach, in which what matters is 
the number of criteria satisfied. Hence, if we establish four criteria with 
similar weighting, and a chatbot meets three of them, we can assign a rat­
ing of 75% for the skill in question.
In this project, we use a mixed approach for the following reason: For 
some skills, it is natural to establish different levels, so a criterion can be 
formulated for the basic level, alongside more advanced ones. For other 
skills, however, specific criteria cannot easily be identified as basic or more 
advanced. At the same time, for such skills it is important to cover various 
operations, making the “flat approach” more appropriate.
On these grounds, we developed a kind of benchmark, which differs in 
some aspects from conventional benchmarks created for assessing the per­
formance of LLMs (see Banerjee et al., 2023). One of most famous bench­
marks used to measure understanding in LLMs is the General Language 
Understanding Evaluation (GLUE) benchmark, introduced in 2018 (Wang 
et al., 2019). It takes into account 21 aspects of understanding, grouped 
into 4 categories: lexical semantics, predicate–argument structure, logic, 
knowledge, and common sense. Subsequently, more challenging bench­
marks have been developed, such as the Beyond the Imitation Game 
Benchmark (BIG-Bench), which includes over 200 diverse text-based 
tasks (Srivastava et al., 2022). The task categories encompass traditional 
natural-language processing, mathematics, common-sense reasoning, and 

--- Page 17 ---

17
2.2. General Assumptions
question-answering. As models evolved, researchers recognised the need 
to focus on the most difficult tasks, leading to, for example, the creation 
of BIG-Bench Hard (BBH): “a subset of 23 challenging BIG-Bench tasks 
on which prior language models fell short of average reported human-
rater performance” to demonstrate that “answer-only prompting under­
estimates model capabilities” and that chain of thought (CoT) prompting 
“enables the most capable Codex model to outperform the average human-
rater baseline on 17 out of 23 tasks in BBH” (Suzgun et al., 2023, p. 13011).
Our benchmark: (1) focuses on a narrow set of skills, specifically theo­
logical ones; (2) relies solely on expert evaluation, and not on a generalised 
result of rating. A preliminary application of this benchmark in simplified 
form was presented in an earlier article reporting the project’s first results 
(Trepczyński, 2023). Below, we present the final version of this benchmark, 
which we designate as the Rational Theologian Benchmark (RT-Bench).
The development of nine theological skills for a rational theologian and 
the determination of assessment criteria for each constituted the basis for 
composing nine groups of tasks. A table of theological skills and assess­
ment criteria is presented in section 2.3, with at least one task assigned to 
each criterion. The rating guidelines for each skill are outlined in section 
2.4. The complete set of tasks is detailed in section 2.5.
Such a simplified methodological framework can be considered as just 
a starting point for further development. However, it turned out to be suf­
ficient for effectively evaluating the skills of LLM-based chatbots in order 
to determine whether they can serve as rational theologians and to enable 
performance comparisons in this respect.
Finally, we should make another important methodological note. Even 
if chatbots demonstrate that they have satisfactory skills and knowledge, 
they may still exhibit biases, including religious ones, and may promote bi­
ases, among them: biases against women (cf. Biana, 2024). Although con­
temporary leading chatbots incorporate instructions designed to guaran­
tee their political correctness and in consequence eliminate a wide range 
of potential biases, they may still present content including biases which 
are hard to detect.

2. Methodology 
2.3. Theological Skills and Assessment Criteria
Table 1 presents the seven core skills for a rational theologian and two 
meta-skills. The latter refer to the concept of a rational theologian and to 
the capacity for evaluating theological skills.
In our view, positive results from testing whether chatbots fulfil such crite­
ria should be sufficient to establish that they can serve as rational theologians.
For each skill, several assessment criteria have been determined, and 
for each criterion: at least one task (presented in section 2.5).

2. Methodology 
2.4. Rating Framework
The assessment is based on a weighted percentage system where each cri­
terion is assigned a predetermined percentage value that contributes to the 
overall skill score, and where all criteria within a skill add up to 100%. Chat­
bots receive the maximum allocated percentage for a criterion when they 
complete all associated tasks flawlessly, but scores are proportionally reduced 
based on the evaluator’s judgment of any identified deficiencies or errors.
The specific percentage allocations for each criterion within the nine as­
sessed skills are detailed below, representing a simplified approach where 
scoring is distributed relatively evenly if the criteria do not overlap, with 
slightly lower or higher maximum scores assigned based on factors such 
as complexity levels and operations connected with creativity being rated 
somewhat higher according to Bloom’s taxonomy.
1. Interprets the Bible:
•	 fulfilling only criterion 1.1: 40%,
•	 1.1 + 1.2: 75%,
•	 1.1 + 1.3: 75%,
•	 1.2 + 1.3: 100%.
•	 Comments: Criterion 1.2 encompasses 1.1. Since the criteria overlap,

2.5. Tasks
In order to measure proficiency in the skills enumerated in section 2.3, a 
collection of tasks was composed, for which we conducted pilot testing. 
Many were presented at conferences for feedback and discussion. Some 
were improved. Others were added at a later stage to complement the pre­
vious collection. The final collection was divided into nine groups. The 
tasks can be used as ready prompts to test the capacities of chatbots.
The full set of tasks is presented below.1 Tasks marked with one asterisk 
or two asterisks are follow-up questions. In some cases, an additional ut­
terance (marked with “ADD”) is provided, which can be used in a prompt 
if we do not get a satisfactory reply, but want to give the chatbot another 
chance. There are also additional tasks, such as T1.6, which were not used 
to measure proficiency in skills listed in section 2.3 but can be useful for 
checking other aspects of chatbots’ performance.
1	
After the chatbot testing was completed, the wording of the tasks was corrected dur­
ing copy-editing to eliminate errors in punctuation, grammar, and style. The changes 
introduced do not alter the meaning of the questions.

“The fact that (1) God is a trinity of persons, follows from the fact that

ture and essence this characteristic, that it begets its splendour from itself. 
(4) The light that begets and the splendour that is begotten necessarily are 
locked in a mutual embrace and breathe out their mutual warmth. (5) The 
begetter and the begotten are either (5.1) something different and someone 
different or (5.2) not something different but someone different, or (5.3) not 
someone different but something different (5.4) neither someone different 
nor something different but in some way different, or (5.5) neither some­
one different nor something different nor in some way different. (6) Of the 
five parts of this division four cannot occur in God and some cannot occur 
either in God or in anything else. (7) It is not possible in any case that the 
begotten is not someone different nor something different, nor in some 
way different, from the begetter. (8) Nor is it ever possible for the begotten 
to be something different from the begetter, but not someone different. 
(9) It is not possible in God that the begotten should be in some way dif­
ferent from the begetter, since ‘in some way different’ means ‘different ac­
cording to some accidental difference.’ (10) Nor, again, is it possible in God 
that the begotten should be something different from the begetter, since 
(11) there is no multiplicity of substances in God. There remains, then, that 
(12) the begotten is not something different from the begetter, but only 
someone different; and (13) the same argument applies to the breather and 
the breathed. (14) There is in God, then, someone, and someone else, and a 
third someone, each of whom is an individual substance of rational nature:

“Third, they disagree on the sin of Peter. For Jerome says that in the dis­

Cyprian, and Paul himself, who plainly teaches that Peter was deserving of 
rebuke. Therefore, if it is unlawful to say that anything false is contained 
in Sacred Scripture, it will not be lawful to say that Peter was not deserving

3. Results: Discussion and 
Assessment
3.1. Interpreting the Bible
In order to check whether two tested chatbots (ChatGPT and Gemini) can 
interpret the Bible, the following tasks were used: T1.1, T1.2, T1.2*, T1.3, 
T1.4, T1.4*, T1.4** and T1.5.
3.1.1. General Hermeneutic Capacities
In T1.1, the chatbots were asked to “interpret and explain the following 
passage: ‘In the year that King Uzziah died, I saw the Lord, high and exalt­
ed, seated on a throne; and the train of his robe filled the temple.’” This re­
quest was preceded by the prompt asking to act as a Christian theologian.
ChatGPT analysed each element of this passage. It correctly identified 
the passage (Isaiah 6:1) and its historical context. It argued that the text 
described a vision “because, according to Jewish thought, no one could 
see God and live,” and from this perspective, the chatbot interpreted all 
the elements of the vision. Finally, it correctly identified some theological 
implications.
Gemini’s answer was more concise than ChatGPT’s. It correctly identi­
fied the passage, as well as its context, meaning, and theological implica­
tions.
Both chatbots can be assigned the maximum score for meeting crite­
rion 1.1, that is, 40%.

--- Page 38 ---

38
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
3.1.2. The Four Senses Theory
In T1.2, the chatbots were asked to apply the theory of the four senses of 
Scripture (literal, moral, allegorical, and anagogical) to interpret the fol­
lowing fragment: “All Israel came together to David at Hebron and said, 
‘We are your own flesh and blood.’” This request was also preceded by 
the condition “If you were a Christian theologian.” According to the four 
senses hermeneutic framework, a reader of the Bible focuses not only on 
the literal layer of the text, but also on the spiritual one (Vawter, 1964; Ro­
szak, 2016; Manresa Lamarca, 2017). The latter includes: the moral sense, 
showing us what to do; the allegorical one, which usually refers to Jesus 
(e.g., Isaac can prefigure Jesus Christ); and the anagogical one, which refers 
to the future glory. The task was intended to check if the chatbots are able 
to interpret the Bible using such a framework.
Both chatbots correctly identified the source of the quotation and for­
mulated possible theological interpretations for each of the four senses, 
which are in line with Christian theology and perfectly fitting to the re­
quirements of each of the four senses. Interestingly, ChatGPT identified 
this quote as 1 Chronicles 11:1, and Gemini as 2 Samuel 5:1. Both were 
right because the two passages have almost the same wording.
Next, the chatbots were given a similar task, but this time the prompt 
did not refer to a biblical passage, but to an invented sentence which imi­
tated the biblical style: “And he sailed far out into the sea, and no waves 
swallowed him up. And he reached the end, the shore that awaited him.” 
The aim of this additional task was to determine whether the correct re­
plies relied on the chatbots’ skills or on knowledge transmitted to the mod­
els during their training.
ChatGPT recognised that the passage was not taken from the Bible, 
stating: “It doesn’t seem to correspond directly to a specific verse from 
Scripture.” However, once again it performed the task perfectly. Similar­
ly, Gemini asserted: “Note: While the specific context of this fragment is 
unclear, I’ll provide a general interpretation based on common themes in 
Christian theology.” Gemini’s performance was equally successful.

--- Page 39 ---

39
3.1. Interpreting the Bible
This means that when interpreting the Bible with the use of a frame­
work such as the four senses theory, the models do not rely on the knowl­
edge obtained during training, but apply advanced hermeneutical skills. 
Thus, with respect to criterion 1.2 they deserve the maximum score.
3.1.3. Using Logic to Understand the Bible
In order to check whether the chatbots can use logic as a tool for interpret­
ing the Bible, in T1.3, they were asked to solve a specific puzzle which was 
discussed by mediaeval theologians. It refers to Jesus’s exchange with a 
Pharisee, as recorded in the Gospel of Luke, including the phrase: “There­
fore, I tell you, her many sins have been forgiven, as she loved much. But 
whoever has been forgiven little loves little” (Luke 7:47). Stephen Langton 
(d. 1228), a Parisian theologian, in his parallel questions 78g and 78b from 
Book II, Volume 2 of his Quaestiones theologiae, noted that Jesus’s words 
might seem inconsistent (Langton, 2022, 192, 198). The first sentence sug­
gests that the woman’s love was the cause of God’s forgiveness and remis­
sion of sins, whereas the second sentence: that love follows forgiveness, thus 
reversing the causal relationship. Moreover, according to Christian the-
ology, God’s grace is unconditional, so the woman’s love cannot be a cause 
of God’s decision or action. Notably, in modern English translations the 
problem no longer exists, since the wording of this phrase has been changed 
to “as her love has shown.” However, the Greek original (ὅτι ἠγάπησεν πολύ) 
and the Latin translation (quoniam dilexit multum) should be translated to: 
“as she loved much.” This problem was briefly presented in the prompt, and 
the chatbots were asked to explain it as Christian theologians.
In its extensive reply, ChatGPT correctly indicated theological rules 
concerning God’s grace, which has no external cause, and correctly solved 
the problem by indicating that the woman’s love was not the cause, but the 
evidence of the forgiveness. As ChatGPT indicated, Jesus “is not teaching 
that her love caused her forgiveness. Instead, her love is a demonstration or 
evidence of the forgiveness she has already received.” Moreover, it referred 
to the Greek connective ὅτι (hoti, translated into English as “as” or “for”) 

--- Page 40 ---

40
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
to indicate that this connective can “also mean ‘because,’ indicating that 
her love is the evidence of her forgiveness.”
Gemini also correctly identified the general theological rules concern­
ing God’s grace, and in conclusion it stated: “The woman’s love serves as 
evidence of her genuine repentance and her gratitude for God’s grace.” 
Thus, it solved the problem.
Next, the chatbots were asked to “use terms from the theory of reasoning 
or logical terms to explain the role of the connective ‘as’ which links the 
two parts of the above-mentioned reasoning,” meaning Jesus’s reasoning: 
“her many sins have been forgiven, as she loved much.”
ChatGPT gave an extensive (possibly even too extensive) reply. It right­
ly pointed out that the connective “as,” in this context, “functions primar­
ily in an evidential or explanatory role, rather than as a marker of direct 
causality.” It also emphasised that the relationship between the two clauses 
in this reasoning “is one-directional,” and cannot be considered bicondi­
tional. As the chatbot adds, “we can see this as an example of abductive 
reasoning, where we infer the occurrence of an unseen cause (forgiveness) 
from an observable effect (love).”
Gemini chose to use the concept of material implication and symbolise 
it with an arrow to present the following formula: “Her many sins have 
been forgiven → She loved much.” It also used the logical concept of suf­
ficient condition to state that, in this context, “forgiveness is a sufficient 
condition for love, but not necessarily the only cause.”
In the next task, the chatbots were first expected to indicate theologians 
who solved that problem in a similar way, and then to focus specifically on 
mediaeval theologians, providing quotations from their works.
ChatGPT enumerated St Augustine, Thomas Aquinas, John Calvin, 
Martin Luther, Karl Barth, N.T. Wright, and Origen. In the majority of 
cases, it indicated the principle that God’s grace always comes first. How­
ever, it noted that Calvin and Wright had commented on the discussed 
fragment from Luke and provided a similar interpretation. ChatGPT’s an­
swer is generally correct; however, it is not very precise. Although it men­
tions Calvin’s commentary on Luke, it does not quote it. It seems impor­
tant to note that Calvin did not discuss the special role of the connective 

--- Page 41 ---

41
3.1. Interpreting the Bible
“as” (quoniam/quia). He only remarked that “it is not here said that the 
loving, or the love of men towards God, is the cause of the forgiveness of 
sins.” Whereas the above-mentioned mediaeval theologian, Stephen Lang­
ton, in question 78c, examines this aspect in detail: “love is given here as 
a sign of love (…), and this is the meaning: greater love is a sign of greater 
love. And the word ‘as’ (quoniam) or ‘because’ (quia) has an ostensive, and 
not a causal function” (Langton, 2022, 201).
When asked to quote mediaeval theologians, ChatGPT mentioned 
Thomas Aquinas and started hallucinating. It wrongly attributed Aqui­
nas’s considerations about love and forgiveness to question 24, article 7 
of part II-II of his Summa theologiae, and gave the following non-existent 
“excerpt” from this article (the Latin original was also consulted), which, 
according to ChatGPT, discussed this issue: “The cause of charity is God, 
Who pours forth charity into our hearts by His grace, as stated above 
(I-II, Q. 24, A. 2). Hence it is clear that the increase of charity results from 
an increase of grace.” What is more, the chatbot failed to provide any quo­
tation in which a theologian would offer a logical explanation of this prob­
lem. Thus, ChatGPT’s usefulness with respect to criterion 1.3 is limited.
Gemini named the following theologians: Augustine of Hippo, Thom­
as Aquinas, and Karl Barth, justifying these choices briefly. However, it 
did not try to convince the human user that these authors gave solutions 
similar to the one formulated by the chatbot. On the contrary, it stated: 
“It’s important to note that while these theologians may not have explic­
itly addressed the specific interpretation I’ve provided, their works offer 
valuable insights into the broader themes of forgiveness, love, and the 
nature of human salvation.” It shows that Gemini 1.5 does not have suf­
ficiently extensive knowledge to give a satisfactory answer. However, the 
chatbot acknowledges its own limitations, which is more valuable than an 
attempt to guess the answer, as this can lead to hallucination. Similarly, in 
the next step, it stated: “While I couldn’t find an exact quote that mirrors 
my interpretation verbatim, here are some excerpts from medieval theo­
logical works that align with the general theme of forgiveness, love, and 
their interconnectedness.” This attempt was unsuccessful. The first “quota­
tion” was claimed to come from Aquinas’s Summa theologiae, part II-II, 

--- Page 42 ---

42
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
question 27, article 5, but it cannot be found there (or anywhere else). Next, 
it referred to St Bonaventure’s Collationes in Hexameron, VII, but although 
the quotation was very close to what we find in the original text, the exact 
formulation does not appear there. Finally, it “quoted” Anselm of Can­
terbury’s Cur Deus homo, but this time without specifying the fragment, 
making it difficult to determine whether such a passage exists in this work, 
and whether it could be Gemini’s translation from Latin. However, if we 
restrict ourselves to English, search engines do not locate such a passage.
This means that at the current stage of development, ChatGPT and 
Gemini still hallucinate when attempting to quote mediaeval theological 
sources. This shows that the chatbots require further improvement in this 
area. The development of the RAG method, already applied in ChatGPT, 
appears promising. However, it is important to provide chatbots with 
mechanisms which guarantee that they find and use only reliable resourc­
es and process selected materials in such a way that content presented as 
quotations remains unmodified. With such safeguards, they may be able 
to quote authors such as mediaeval theologians correctly. Let us add that 
if we want to develop a model which will operate on works created by a 
specific author, fine-tuning the model on a corpus of that author’s writ­
ings is also an option. Unfortunately, the risk of hallucination remains. 
However, as proven by Banelli and Skelac, who trained Llama-3 on the cor­
pus of Benedict’s XVI texts, such a fine-tuned model can indeed replicate 
the author’s style and improve its skills, for example, it can better evaluate 
theological argumentation (Banelli & Skelac, 2024).
Finally, the chatbots were asked to express in terms of formal or sym­
bolic logic the statement “I am in the Father and the Father is in me,” which 
comes from John 14:11.
ChatGPT pointed out the “relation of indwelling or mutual contain­
ment between Jesus (J) and the Father (F)” and proposed to denote the 
relationship “is in” using the symbol R(x, y), which means “x is in y.” In 
conclusion, it stated that “I am in the Father, and the Father is in me” can 
be represented as the conjunction of both relations: R(J, F) ∧ R(F, J).
Initially, Gemini offered two symbols for the two parts of that sentence 
and produced the following formula: “P ∧ Q.” It was then asked to repre­

--- Page 43 ---

43
3.1. Interpreting the Bible
sent the basic clauses in terms of relations. Gemini introduced the basic 
terms (“I” and “Father”) and two relations, to finally merge them into one: 
R = R1 ∩ R2 = {(I, in), (the Father, in)}, and in conclusion, to rightly state 
that both relations are in fact one relation of being in. We can say that it 
performed well; however, ChatGPT was more effective this time.
In the second part of this task, the chatbots were asked to give a similar 
formal representation of the reasoning formulated by Thomas Aquinas in 
his commentary on John 14:11. The reasoning was extensively quoted in 
the prompt, but it was not attributed to Aquinas. ChatGPT performed the 
task very well. It defined all the terms and relations to finally provide the 
following formal representation of the given reasoning:
E(F) = F and E(S) = S (essence is the same as the person in reality).
R(E(F), y) → R(F, y) and R(E(S), y) → R(S, y) (where the essence is, the 
person is).
R(E(F), S) and R(E(S), F) (the essence of the Father is in the Son, and 
the essence of the Son is in the Father).
From 2 and 3, we conclude: R(F, S) (the Father is in the Son) and 
R(S, F) (the Son is in the Father).
ChatGPT’s answer is a correct formal representation of the reasoning, 
demonstrating significant progress of the model powered by GPT-4o com­
pared to the previous version, based on GPT-3.5. In the pilot test, Bing 
Chat (based on GPT-4) also performed the task well, whereas ChatGPT 
powered by GPT-3.5 introduced formulas which did not reflect statements 
included in the reasoning.
Now, Gemini correctly introduced basic terms and step by step came 
to the key formulas: ∀x(E(F) = x → F = x), and ∀x(E(S) = x → S = x), and to 
the conclusion: F is in S, S is in F. However, at one point Gemini made a 
mistake, namely, it translated the sentence “Now the essence of the Father 
is in the Son, and the essence of the Son is in the Father” into the following 
erroneous formula: “E(F) is in E(S), E(S) is in E(F),” instead of “E(F) is in 
S, E(S) is in F.” Hence, Gemini still makes mistakes in simple logical tasks.

--- Page 44 ---

44
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
To sum up, the initial task (concerning the connective “as”) was per­
formed perfectly by both chatbots. In their responses to the additional 
questions (i.e., naming other authors who addressed the problem of this 
connective in the quoted passage), the chatbots started hallucinating, so 
their ratings should be reduced. The final task (concerning the formal rep­
resentation of a sentence from the Bible and of a reasoning based on this 
sentence) was performed perfectly by ChatGPT. Gemini encountered mi­
nor issues and made a mistake, so its rating should be reduced.
There was also an important additional observation. In the follow-up 
task (T1.6), the chatbots were asked to give a formal representation of the 
statement “I am an object and I do not exist.” Interestingly, the answers of 
both chatbots revealed their philosophical bias towards Bertrand Russell’s 
views, which were challenged by Terence Parsons (1980), according to whom 
there are only existent objects, or, in other words: if something is an object, 
it exists. ChatGPT claimed that the statement “I am an object and I do not 
exist” “presents a paradox, as it asserts both the existence of a subject (‘I am 
an object’) and the negation of that subject’s existence (‘I do not exist’).” 
Similarly, Gemini remarked that the analysed statement “presents a logical 
contradiction,” because “it asserts that something (an object) both exists 
and does not exist simultaneously.” The dataset used to train these models 
probably included materials which strongly advocated such an approach.
3.2. Clarification of Theological Problems
To check whether the chatbots can clarify theological problems, the fol­
lowing tasks have been used: for criterion 2.1: T2.1, T2.2; for criterion 2.2: 
T2.3; for criterion 2.3: T2.4, T2.5.
3.2.1. Identifying the Core of the Problem
In order to test whether the chatbots can identify the core of a theo­
logical problem, they were given T2.1, which refers to a biblical passage: 

--- Page 45 ---

45
3.2. Clarification of Theological Problems
Romans 9:6–13. This fragment is relatively difficult to understand, because 
it combines two interpretative layers: the first one is St Paul the Apostle’s 
argumentation; the second comprises stories from Genesis about God’s 
promise given to Abraham and about the different situations of two broth­
ers, Jacob and Esau. The chatbots were asked to determine whether this 
fragment relates to the problem of predestination and, if so, to explain how.
ChatGPT identified the passage as “Romans 9:6–12” and step by step 
clarified its meaning. It mentioned: the idea of true Israel, God’s sover­
eign will, and the distinction between children of physical descent and 
“children of the promise,” to emphasise the selectiveness of God’s choices, 
which it combined with the idea of predestination. Finally, it highlighted 
the example of Jacob and Esau as “especially relevant to the doctrine of 
predestination,” and argued for this claim.
Gemini identified the passage (“Romans 9:6–13”) and stated that it is 
“directly related to the problem of predestination.” It defined the term pre­
destination and indicated how the analysed fragment is connected to that 
problem, including aspects such as: free will, justice, and human responsi­
bility. However, it did not refer to any situation or any character from that 
passage. An additional question was therefore asked: “But how is this re­
lated to Abraham and Sarah?” In its reply, Gemini showed that the key ele­
ment linking these characters and the problem in question is the idea of the 
“promise” given by God to Abraham, concerning his spiritual offspring, 
and that there is a connection between this promise and God’s “choice of 
certain individuals for salvation (predestination).” However, Gemini did 
not choose to develop the biblical stories further and did not discuss the 
story of Esau and Jacob. In the case of Gemini, this task revealed a limited 
capacity for sufficiently explaining the relationship between the given pas­
sage and the indicated theological problem. However, Gemini is capable of 
indicating such a relationship, if we ask for it explicitly.
In the next step (T2.2), the chatbots were asked to explain what is proble-
matic or difficult about predestination. Both ChatGPT and Gemini offered 
a list of issues and discussed the key ones in greater detail. Their answers 
were correct, and the problems were presented very well.

--- Page 46 ---

46
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
3.2.2. Solving Theological Problems
In the next task (T2.3), the chatbots were expected to rationally solve the 
problem of predestination from the perspective of a Christian Catholic 
theologian. In order to get a specific solution, and not just an overview 
of different approaches, it is important to specify a particular denomina­
tion. The Catholic approach appeared to present the greatest interpretative 
challenges with respect to predestination. Consequently, this denomina­
tion was selected for analysis.
ChatGPT noted that considering this problem from the angle of a 
Christian Catholic theologian requires “balancing the affirmation of God’s 
sovereignty with the Church’s long-standing teachings on human free will, 
grace, and moral responsibility.” It rightly added that “Catholic theology, 
guided by Scripture, tradition, and key Church councils, navigates this 
tension by emphasizing the cooperation between God’s grace and human 
freedom, while rejecting extreme forms of predestination that would un­
dermine either aspect.” The chatbot also mentioned Calvinism and Pela­
gianism as two opposing approaches rejected by Catholic theology. Next, 
it discussed several topics crucial for addressing this problem from the 
Catholic perspective. It concluded as follows: “Catholic theology offers a 
rational resolution to the problem of predestination by: 1. Affirming both 
God’s sovereignty and human freedom. 2. Emphasizing that God desires 
the salvation of all. 3. Teaching that God’s grace is offered to all, but hu­
mans must freely cooperate with that grace. 4. Rejecting the idea of double 
predestination and affirming that damnation is a consequence of human 
rejection of God, not divine predetermination. 5. Encouraging believers to 
trust in God’s justice and mercy, acknowledging that while predestination 
is a mystery, God’s love and fairness are never compromised.” It seems 
that it is exactly the solution that a rationally thinking Catholic theologian 
would have given or at least: with which they would have agreed.
Gemini, in the concluding paragraph of its reply, stated that “Catho­
lic theology approaches the problem of predestination by emphasizing the 
interconnectedness of God’s sovereignty, human freedom, divine grace, 
divine justice, and the universality of salvation.” In its answer, the chatbot 

--- Page 47 ---

47
3.2. Clarification of Theological Problems
correctly discussed each of these aspects. The key sentences which consti­
tuted the right solution to the problem were: “While God may predestine 
individuals for salvation, it is through his grace that they are enabled to 
cooperate with his will and achieve eternal life. (…) While God may have a 
plan for our lives, we are still free to make choices and respond to his grace. 
(…) While God may choose some for salvation and others for condemna­
tion, this choice is based on his eternal wisdom and justice, not on arbi­
trary whim. (…) God desires the salvation of all, and his grace is available 
to all who seek it.” The penultimate sentence was seemingly intended as a 
defence of God’s justice. However, its proximity to Pelagianism prompted 
a follow-up question to Gemini: whether this implied that God, in his wis­
dom, foresees human behaviour, and on this basis decides whether or not 
to bestow grace. Gemini objected, and thus it confirmed its right under­
standing of the Catholic standpoint.
To sum up, both chatbots gave correct solutions to the problem.
3.2.3. Indicating Objections
Finally, in T2.4, the chatbots were asked to formulate possible objections 
to the solutions to the problem of predestination that they had previously 
proposed, and next, in T2.5: to consider the assumptions of those solu­
tions, in order to indicate weak points that a rational thinker should not 
accept.
ChatGPT produced eight objections, and in this way it indicated the 
potential weaknesses of the offered solution. It correctly referenced the 
sources of some critisicms (such as Calvin’s opinions) and important 
theological positions (such as Pelagianism). It also included an objection 
which can stem from reading the Bible. Finally, it highlighted the fact that 
some “might argue that appealing to mystery is more of a retreat from 
the problem than a true resolution.” What is most important, in its re­
ply, it addressed the key problems that philosophers and theologians see in 
this solution. In the next step, it correctly pointed out the main assump­
tions of the solution, and indicated its weak points and their inherent ra­

--- Page 48 ---

48
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
tional challenges. We should emphasise that these challenges are indeed 
ones that come to mind when rationally examining those assumptions, 
although they are very similar to the previously presented objections. 
ChatGPT’s conclusion also deserves attention: “a rational thinker might 
find some of the assumptions difficult to accept, especially regarding the 
tension between foreknowledge and free will, the appeal to mystery, and 
the possibility of rejecting sufficient grace.” At the same time, it added: 
“That said, none of these weaknesses are necessarily ‘fatal’ in the sense that 
they render the solution irrational or unworthy of belief. Much depends 
on one’s philosophical and theological starting points.” This means that, 
according to ChatGPT, despite its weaknesses and challenges, the Catholic 
solution is not irrational. Moreover, in the first paragraph it asserted that 
this approach is “balanced and rational.”
Gemini’s reply was more concise. It focused on the philosophical and 
scientific approach, and it hardly addressed the theological one. The chat­
bot presented four objections. It pointed out that “some critics might argue 
that my solution leans too heavily on the concept of divine grace, effectively 
reducing human free will to a mere illusion.” It also indicated the problem 
of unfairness, as the Catholic solution “implies that some individuals are 
inherently more deserving of salvation than others.” Finally, as potential 
objections it mentioned the incompatibility of this solution with science 
(“the idea of a divine grace that precedes human choice is incompatible 
with the notion of a universe governed by natural laws”) and the lack of 
empirical evidence (“there is no empirical evidence to support the exist­
ence of divine grace or the idea of predestination”). In fact, the last two 
objections undermine Christian theology in general, because it includes 
many key statements which are not empirically verifiable, with some pre­
cluding the strong determinism of nature. However, Gemini is right that 
such objections can occur also in the debate on predestination. In the next 
step, Gemini correctly pointed out four assumptions of the given solution, 
that is: (1) the existence of God; (2) the existence of divine grace; (3) the 
existence of human free will; (4) the compatibility of divine sovereignty 
and human freedom. Finally, it listed out the weak points of the solution by 
repeating the previously presented objections. In the concluding remarks, 

--- Page 49 ---

49
3.3. Critical Assessment of Theological Reasoning
it stated: “As a theologian, I believe that my solution offers a reasonable and 
coherent approach to the problem, but I also recognize the limitations and 
challenges it faces.” Thus, similarly to ChatGPT, Gemini highlighted the 
rational aspect of the solution.
Both chatbots performed the task well. At the same time, they produced 
very different content by listing different assumptions for the Catholic so­
lution and by enumerating different sets of objections and weak points. 
This suggests that we can obtain a more complete answer if we merge both 
responses. Does this mean that the answers were incomplete? To some ex­
tent, the answer must be positive, especially if we consider the assump­
tions, where nothing essential should be omitted. However, one can argue 
that some things should be obvious, and it is impossible to enumerate all 
assumptions. If so, when answering the question about assumptions, one 
must select those deemed most important. The same applies to potential 
objections or weaknesses of a given solution. If we adopt this approach, we 
can conclude that both chatbots deserve the maximum score for this task.
3.3. Critical Assessment of Theological Reasoning
The skill of critically assessing theological reasoning was tested using the 
following tasks: for criterion 3.1: T3.1, T3.2, T3.4, and T3.7; for criterion 
3.2: T3.2 and T3.3; for criterion 3.3: T3.2, T3.2*, T3.3, T3.4, T3.5, T3.6, and 
T3.6*; for criterion 3.4: T3.1, T3.2, and T3.6.
3.3.1. Premises and Conclusions
In T3.1, the chatbots were asked to explain St Paul’s argumentation from 
Romans 9:6–13, and to point out the claim of the argumentation (conclu­
sion) and the way St Paul supports it.
In an extensive answer, ChatGPT correctly identified the main claim: 
“God’s promises have not failed, even though not all ethnic Israelites are 
part of God’s chosen people in the spiritual sense.” It also indicated that 

--- Page 50 ---

50
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
“Paul supports this claim with examples from Israel’s patriarchal history, 
showing that God’s relationship with His people has always been based on 
His sovereign choice, not mere lineage or human effort.” Next, ChatGPT 
presented each example, showing how it relates to the claim and how it 
justifies it. The task was completed successfully.
Gemini also indicated the claim correctly: “St. Paul is asserting that 
God’s promise of salvation is not based solely on physical lineage or human 
merit, but rather on God’s sovereign choice.” It also rightly demonstrated 
how St Paul supports this claim, by referring to the distinction between the 
physical descendants and the children of the promise and showing how the 
examples given by Paul prove God’s sovereign election predetermines sal­
vation. The task was executed perfectly, and the response was more concise 
than in the case of ChatGPT.
The chatbots were also asked to indicate the premises and conclusion 
in T3.2. While the previous task was based on argumentation from the 
Bible, which could have been present in the training sets of both chat­
bots, probably including some commentaries on this biblical passage, T3.2 
was different. It referred to reasoning found in Stephen Langton’s com­
mentary on 1 Chronicles 4:9, which mentions Jabez, whose name means 
“pain.” Langton’s commentary on Chronicles has only been published in 
Latin and this edition has not been digitised yet (Stephen Langton, 1978, 
213). Hence, to perform the task, the chatbots operated on a previously 
unknown text. Moreover, the reasoning is challenging to analyse due of 
the presence of certain indicators—such as “hence”—which seem to be 
introducing a conclusion, while the actual conclusion appears elsewhere, 
without such a marker. Additionally, the connections between the state­
ments which constitute the reasoning are difficult to trace. Despite these 
ambiguities, ChatGPT successfully identified the conclusion: “Jabez was 
born from his mother with pain because such a good cannot be obtained 
without a bitterness of heart.” It also correctly listed the premises, at the 
same time showing which of them are directly linked with the conclusion 
and which support other premises.

--- Page 51 ---

51
3.3. Critical Assessment of Theological Reasoning
Gemini also found the conclusion and quoted the same sentence as 
ChatGPT. It also correctly listed the premises; however, it did not differen­
tiate between them.
In T3.4, the chatbots continued T3.3, in which they were asked to indi­
cate the logical connection between a general theological statement and a 
statement taken from the Bible. In T3.4, they attempted to reconstruct the 
reasoning which links the given statements. In their answers, both chat­
bots correctly identified the premises and conclusions of the inferences 
they had carried out.
Finally, in T3.7 the chatbots were asked to create a diagram of the rea­
soning given in T3.6 (Robert Grosseteste’s proof of the Trinity based on the 
assumption that God is light; see more in section 3.3.3; Robert Grosseteste, 
1999, 224), which explored linked and convergent premises. If they were 
unable to generate a diagram, they were instructed to present the reason­
ing in a different format.
ChatGPT chose to describe the relationship between the statements 
contained in the reasoning in words. It indicated the main conclusion cor­
rectly (“God is a Trinity of persons”). It also managed to identify linked 
premises and the relationships between them, as well as one situation 
where convergent premises occur.
Gemini failed this task. It attempted to label premises and conclusion, 
but at the same time it used vertical arrows, which suggest the progress of 
the reasoning. However, it labelled the main conclusion (“God is a trinity 
of persons”) as Premise 1, from which Premise 2 proceeds (“God is light”), 
which is completely wrong.
To conclude, both chatbots are quite capable of identifying the prem­
ises and conclusions of theological argumentation, or of pinpointing their 
main claims and the ways in which they are supported. They proved it 
also when working with highly challenging material taken from Langton’s 
commentary. However, when attempting to create a diagram, Gemini fo­
cused too heavily on this part of the task and incorrectly identified the 
elements of the reasoning. Thus, it revealed its limitations in this scope, 
when distracted by an additional difficult task. Due to this fact, it should 
receive a lower score.

--- Page 52 ---

52
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
We should also note that the latest versions of ChatGTP try to generate 
images. When this task was repeated at a later time, the chatbot produced 
an absolutely unacceptable image that it presented as a diagram. The out­
put was incoherent and unintelligible. This shows that diagramming is still 
a capacity not yet mastered by chatbots.
3.3.2. Logical Connections
In T3.2, the chatbots were asked to indicate premises, conclusions, reason­
ing markers (connectives such as “therefore”) and logical connections in 
a reasoning concerning Jabez, and finally to assess the subsequent steps of 
the reasoning.
ChatGPT correctly indicated the markers and, as it was previously stat­
ed, the premises and conclusions. It also accurately designated the connec­
tions between the statements which constitute the reasoning.
Gemini also correctly identified reasoning markers (unlike ChatGPT, 
it did not include “namely,” but this is acceptable, since the classification of 
“namely” as a reasoning marker is open to discussion), as well as premises 
and conclusions. Next, it accurately explained how the key elements of the 
reasoning are connected.
In T3.3, the chatbots were asked whether they see logical connections 
between the statement “It is very good for those in power to have an honest 
man by their side, whose presence they respect” and the following sentence 
from the Bible: “Stand in the multitude of the elders; and cleave unto him 
that is wise.” Both chatbots confirmed that they see a logical connection 
between the given statements and correctly argued for their solutions.
To sum up, both chatbots are perfectly capable of indicating connec­
tions between the elements of a theological reasoning.
3.3.3. Validity Assessment
Tasks T3.2, T3.3, and T3.4, already used to evaluate the previous criteria, 
also serve to verify whether the chatbots can assess the validity of logical 

--- Page 53 ---

53
3.3. Critical Assessment of Theological Reasoning
connections between the statements of a reasoning (criterion 3.3). Addi­
tionally, for this criterion, we will use: T3.2*, T3.5, T3.6, and T3.6*.
In its answer to T3.2, ChatGPT checked each step of the reasoning 
concerning Jabez with respect to its validity. For example, the statement 
labelled by ChatGPT as Premise 2 (“Pain is more honorable than his broth­
ers”) was described by the chatbot as making “a value judgment, which 
requires justification.” Next, it explained: “The reasoning here is that pain 
brings about good things, particularly a sorrow that leads to repentance 
(2 Cor. 7:10). This is a valid theological interpretation, as godly sorrow 
is indeed considered spiritually beneficial in Christian teaching.” Thus, 
ChatGPT referred to the concept of validity, but in a specific sense, namely, 
within the framework of theological interpretation, and not in terms of 
logic. Regarding the statement labelled as Premise 3 (“The gold of Havilah 
is the best”), ChatGPT pointed out the logical gap (see below). Finally, ac­
cording to the chatbot, Premise 4 (“The heart of the wise is where there is 
mourning”) “effectively supports the notion that sorrow or pain can lead 
to wisdom, and by extension, honor” and “provides strong support for the 
argument.” As the chatbot stated: “The reasoning relies heavily on meta­
phorical connections between pain, sorrow, wisdom, and spiritual hon­
or. While the logic is sound within its theological framework, the jump 
from pain to spiritual honor (via sorrow leading to repentance) makes 
sense.” It also pointed out another problematic step, which is linking the 
statement that pain does many good things with the statement “we read 
in Genesis (2:11–12) that the gold of Havilah is the best,” which is intro­
duced by the connective “hence,” which suggests a logical consequence. 
ChatGPT rightly indicated that “the connection with Genesis 2:11–12 (the 
gold of Havilah) is somewhat obscure and not entirely clear in its logical 
relevance. This premise seems weak or underdeveloped in the argument.” 
This observation is very accurate, as the connection is indeed vague. Such 
a form of connection seems to be characteristic of biblical commentaries. 
It has been designated as “special biblical inference” and conceptualised as 
a type of reasoning in which reason–consequence and premise–conclusion 
relationships are indeterminate. In this context, one may speak of a certain 
entanglement between statements rather than a strict logical connection 

--- Page 54 ---

54
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
(cf. Trepczyński & Będkowski, 2024: 213–215). Moreover, ChatGPT is also 
right when it mentions that the premise used in this step seems “weak or 
underdeveloped,” as it is hard to see the parallel between the honour of 
pain or the good results it brings and the special status of the gold of Hav­
ilah. Finally, ChatGPT claims that “the overall reasoning works within its 
scriptural context, showing that pain or sorrow is often a path to higher 
spiritual goods, such as honor, wisdom, or salvation.” It also adds: “There­
fore, the conclusion about Jabez being born in pain and the connection to 
honor is reasonable, though it relies heavily on interpreting pain positively 
in a spiritual sense.” This means that if we adopt a framework in which we 
accept metaphorical reading and a spiritual context within which pain or 
sorrow can be positive, the result of the reasoning is correct. In conclu­
sion, ChatGPT claims that despite the above-mentioned gap, “the overall 
reasoning is valid, supported by scriptural references, and flows logically 
from the premises to the conclusion.”
On the one hand, ChatGPT carried out a convincing assessment of the 
reasoning. On the other hand, the outcome of this assessment is question-
able. Notably, ChatGPT’s evaluation concentrated on determining whether 
the inference is reasonable, that is, whether the conclusion could be drawn 
from the premises within the given framework. However, it did not identi­
fy any lacking premises, specifically implications (if p then q), which would 
be necessary to obtain a complete deductive reasoning. The absence of such 
premises renders the reasoning incomplete and thus: incorrect from the 
formal point of view. This suggests that ChatGPT opted for a substantial 
assessment rather than a formal one. Consequently, it became necessary to 
examine whether it is capable of conducting a formal assessment. Hence, 
in T3.2*, it was asked to identify the reasoning according to the classi­
fication of reasoning proposed by Tadeusz Czeżowski, who developed a 
classification elaborated by logicians from the Lvov-Warsaw School, in 
which deductive (inference, proving) and reductive (explanation, testing) 
types of reasoning were distinguished.
ChatGPT correctly presented this classification and, in its light, ana­
lysed the reasoning concerning Jabez. It pointed out that “The argument 
about Jabez does not directly involve a formal logical deduction from axi­

--- Page 55 ---

55
3.3. Critical Assessment of Theological Reasoning
oms or clearly defined premises in a strict mathematical sense. Rather, it 
seeks to explain the meaning of Jabez’s name and the symbolic significance 
of pain, sorrow, and spiritual honor.” In conclusion, it stated: “The reason­
ing fits into reductive reasoning and more specifically into the subcategory 
of explanation. It seeks to provide a theological and scriptural explanation 
for why Jabez (associated with pain) is considered honorable, rather than 
attempting a formal deduction or a process of empirical testing.” ChatGPT 
seems to be right. This reasoning is in fact a kind of explanation. It is also 
not a deductive reasoning, which means that, although it can be reason­
able, it is not based on any deductive scheme, and it lacks axioms or clearly 
defined premises which would enable applying such a scheme. To sum up, 
this additional task provided an opportunity to supplement the assessment 
with the formal approach. However, ChatGPT had to be prompted to apply 
this method, as it did not undertake a formal analysis on its own initiative. 
For this reason, despite the overall quality of its evaluation, it should not 
be awarded a high rating.
Gemini stated that the analysed reasoning is flawed and showed its er­
rors. In conclusion, it said: “the reasoning presented is not logically sound. 
It contains errors in equivocation, non-sequitur, begging the question, and 
false analogy. While the passage may have spiritual or symbolic signifi­
cance, it does not hold up to logical scrutiny.” Gemini adopted a strictly 
logical perspective, which is very different from the approach represented 
by ChatGPT, which used a theological framework to read the reasoning 
and verify its plausibility. Gemini rejected this framework, interpreting 
the spiritual reading of pain as a case of the fallacy of equivocation. Rather 
than seeking coherence between the statements, it focused on determin­
ing whether any logical consequence could be established. Finding none, 
it rightly indicated the “non sequitur” fallacy, as well as the fallacy of beg­
ging the question. The chatbots were asked to show the logical connections 
between the statements and assess the correctness of the subsequent steps 
of the reasoning. In my view, such an assessment should first include the 
formal correctness of a reasoning. If so, Gemini performed the task better 
than ChatGPT. Thus, it should receive a higher rating for this task. How-
ever, at the same time, it is valuable to indicate special frameworks within 

--- Page 56 ---

56
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
which a reasoning is acceptable. It is valuable because it shows that using 
reasoning can help us better understand the sense of some statements 
(here: a passage from the Bible). This is what ChatGPT did. By contrast, 
when reasoning is dismissed solely on the basis of fallacies, the implication 
is often that it should be refuted, whereas in specific contexts, such as theo­
logical interpretation, it may still hold value. Thus, the limited approach 
represented by Gemini may be considered incomplete.
This approach was somewhat complemented thanks to T3.2*. Gemini 
also identified the reasoning type as explanation. As it stated: “The reason­
ing attempts to explain why Jabez was ‘more honorable than his brothers’ 
by connecting his name to the concept of ‘pain’ and its associated qual­
ities. It aims to provide a reason or cause for his character, rather than 
proving or inferring anything about him.” In this way, Gemini both cor­
rectly classified the reasoning and indicated its role, showing that there 
is some meaningful connection (giving a reason or cause for Jabez’s 
character), although the reasoning is reductive (hence: fallible). To sum 
up, although Gemini’s answer may appear incomplete, the chatbot per­
formed the task very well, as logical correctness constitutes the primary 
criterion of assessment. Moreover, Gemini indicated the core of the rea­
soning: that it represents an attempt to justify a given fact. Hence, Gemini 
acknowledged the underlying rationale of the enterprise, while rejecting 
its result on the grounds of logical incorrectness. Thus, if not the maxi­
mum, Gemini should receive a very high rating for this set of tasks.
T3.3 was used as a supplementary task for criterion 3.3. In this task, 
the chatbots were asked to identify the logical connection between a given 
general statement (“It is very good for those in power to have an honest 
man by their side, whose presence they respect”) and a statement taken 
from the Bible (“Stand in the multitude of the elders; and cleave unto him 
that is wise”) in terms of the reason–consequence relation.
ChatGPT stated that “the Biblical statement is the reason or guiding 
principle, while the statement about those in power is the consequence or 
practical application of that principle.” The chatbot extensively explained 
its position in a satisfactory manner by showing how one sentence can be 
deductively inferred from another. In this way, ChatGPT confirmed the 

--- Page 57 ---

57
3.3. Critical Assessment of Theological Reasoning
validity of the reasoning, proceeding from the sentence taken from the 
Bible to the given statement.
Gemini gave a very similar answer to the one provided by ChatGPT 
and a convincing explanation for the solution. In the concluding sentence, 
Gemini stated: “This connection can be seen as a practical application of 
the biblical advice to associate with wise individuals.”
It is worth mentioning that during the pilot testing Gemini’s predeces­
sor, Bard, offered a different answer to the same task: “The first statement 
could be seen as the reason for the second statement. The second statement 
is a specific instruction that is based on the general principle stated in the 
first statement.” This means that the more extensive training which gave 
us the Gemini model resulted in greater compliance between ChatGPT 
and Gemini. However, we should note that Bard’s answer was more nu­
anced. It added that “the two statements could also be seen as two different 
perspectives on the same issue.” It also pointed out that “the relationship 
between the two statements is a matter of interpretation”; that “there is no 
one right answer”; and that the two statements “complement each other.” 
This remark perfectly aligns with the idea of special biblical inference. In­
deed, this task is based on the example of a reasoning identified as special 
biblical inference (cf. Trepczyński & Będkowski, 2024: 213). Thus, it seems 
that Bard’s more moderate answer was also more sensitive. While the latest 
chatbots concentrated on providing a definitive answer, Bard tried to grasp 
the nature of the connection between the statements. One could argue, 
then, that despite advances in LLMs, this represents a step backward. On 
the one hand, ChatGPT and Gemini proved that they can identify logical 
connections between such statements and give good reasons for their solu­
tions. On the other hand, they overlooked the hidden potential of the rela­
tionship between the two statements. In this light, although both chatbots 
deserve a relatively high rating, it is also fair to say that expectations were 
not fully met, so the rating may need to be slightly reduced.
In T3.4, which is a follow-up to T3.3, the chatbots were asked: “Can 
you build a logically correct deductive reasoning which represents one of 
the possible interpretations that you have mentioned?” This task revealed 
that ChatGPT struggles with this kind of logical reconstruction. It should 

--- Page 58 ---

58
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
have built a syllogism in which the sentence taken from the Bible functions 
as a premise, and the statement that it is very good for those in power to 
have an honest man by their side, whose presence they respect, serves as 
a conclusion. ChatGPT treated both sentences as premises. Moreover, the 
premises it selected did not precisely correspond to the two statements that 
should have been linked by a syllogism. Premise 1 is an interpretation of 
the sentence from the Bible, whereas Premise 2 is a statement supplement­
ed with an additional clause. Finally, as a conclusion the chatbot proposed 
a new sentence: “those in power who have an honest and wise advisor by 
their side make better decisions and grow in moral integrity.” Importantly, 
this conclusion does not strictly follow from the premises. The reasoning is 
close to a syllogistic form, but does not fully conform to it.
Similarly, Gemini produced a reasoning which only imitates a syllogism. 
Although it asserted that “this deductive reasoning follows a classic syllogis­
tic form, where the conclusion logically follows from the given premises,” 
the reasoning it provided is not a syllogism. Also in this case, the original 
sentences were reformulated, resulting in a change in meaning.
As Gemini was so confident in the syllogistic form of the reasoning it 
had produced, it was assigned an additional task: T3.5. The chatbot was 
asked to assess whether the reasoning it had formulated was correct. The 
answer was even more disappointing. Gemini presented the scheme of mo­
dus ponendo ponens (if P then Q, P is true, therefore Q is true), defined P 
and Q, and claimed that “The reasoning correctly applies this form, mak­
ing it a valid deduction.” Of course, when a reasoning follows that struc­
ture, it constitutes a valid deduction. However, the reasoning previously 
presented by Gemini does not include an implication. Moreover, if we ap­
ply P and Q, as defined by Gemini, to the modus ponendo ponens scheme, 
we obtain a completely different reasoning from the one originally pro­
vided. Thus, Gemini’s response appears misleading.
ChatGPT was not assigned T3.5, as it had already evaluated its reason­
ing in the answer to T3.4. It stated: “This reasoning is deductively valid be­
cause the conclusion logically follows from the premises. If both premises 
are accepted as true, the conclusion must be true.” The problem is that the 

--- Page 59 ---

59
3.3. Critical Assessment of Theological Reasoning
given reasoning did not have a syllogistic structure, and the conclusion did 
not logically follow from the given premises.
To sum up, while the chatbots can assess the validity of reasoning, they 
struggle with constructing deductive reasoning which would incorporate 
specific given sentences. This means that they do not effectively assess the 
validity of their own reasoning. Therefore, their rating of criterion 3.3 
should be further reduced.
In T3.6, the chatbots were asked whether Robert Grosseteste’s reason­
ing, in which he proves that God is a trinity of persons, is correct. The 
reasoning was quoted in the prompt in full. Additionally, its elements were 
marked with numbers.
ChatGPT carefully analysed the reasoning step by step. In several in­
stances, it rightly identified weaknesses related to the analogy between 
bodily light and God who is, according to Christian theology, incompre­
hensible. It seems the chatbot was also right that “the light analogy can 
only serve as an illustrative tool, not as a definitive proof.” ChatGPT also 
recognised the presence of reasoning by elimination. Finally, after this ex­
tensive analysis, it concluded as follows: “The reasoning is logically correct 
within the bounds of theological analogy and metaphysical argumenta­
tion.” ChatGPT rightly assessed the validity of the argument by showing 
that it is logically correct, despite some premises (based on analogy) being 
questionable. The task was performed perfectly.
Gemini identified five problems related to Robert Grosseteste’s reason­
ing. First, equivocation and analogy. Gemini claims that the term “light” 
is used in both a literal and metaphorical sense, which can “lead to confu­
sion and ambiguity in the argument.” However, it seems that Grosseteste 
does not treat the sentence “God is light” as a metaphor. Perhaps, when the 
Bible says that God is light, this could or should be read metaphorically.
