However, when analysing the reasoning provided by a particular author, 
it is essential to approach it within the author’s framework. Next, Gemini 
claims that the analogy between God and light “may not be entirely ac­
curate or sufficient to explain the nature of the Trinity.” Here, the chatbot 
identified the same problem as ChatGPT. The next issue is that the reason­
ing “relies on several assumptions, such as the idea that God is light and 

--- Page 60 ---

60
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
that the properties of light can be applied to the divine nature.” However, 
in this case, Gemini questions the premises and not the correctness of the 
reasoning. Finally, the chatbot characterised the reasoning as circular. As 
Gemini stated: “Some arguments may be circular, where the conclusion 
is assumed in the premises. For example, assuming that God is a Trinity 
to explain why God is light.” However, it should be noted that Grosseteste 
does not presuppose that God is a Trinity to explain why God is light. In 
this respect, Gemini’s interpretation is fundamentally flawed. Moreover, 
it is difficult to justify the claim that the reasoning is circular. Addition­
ally, Gemini pointed to the allegedly limited perspective of the reasoning, 
explaining that it “seems to focus on the ontological nature of the Trinity, 
neglecting other aspects of the doctrine, such as the economic Trinity (the 
Trinity in relation to the world).” This remark is entirely misplaced. If the 
objective is to prove the Trinity based on the premise that God is light, 
the reasoning should be limited to the necessary steps, without address­
ing any additional problems. In the end, Gemini stated that the reasoning 
“is not logically sound or conclusive,” and added that “the use of analogy, 
assumptions, and potential circular reasoning limit its effectiveness in ex­
plaining the Trinity.” However, it seems that Gemini was right only in one 
of its claims: when it referred to the analogy between light and God which 
can indeed be problematic. The remaining analysis is entirely flawed. Fur­
thermore, while the use of analogy may raise doubts about the premises, 
it does not affect the validity of the reasoning. Gemini should have recog­
nised that the reasoning is logically valid. This means that, this time, the 
chatbot completely failed to assess the validity of the reasoning.
T3.6* was a follow-up task in which the chatbots were asked to identify 
the type of Grosseteste’s reasoning based on the classification provided by 
Czeżowski (similarly to T3.2*). It provided an opportunity to complement 
the assessment of validity from a different perspective.
ChatGPT asserted that the reasoning “is best categorized as reductive 
reasoning, specifically explanation, according to Tadeusz Czeżowski’s clas­
sification.” Although previously ChatGPT had assessed that the reasoning 
was correct, this time the chatbot said that it was “not strictly deductive 
because it does not rely on formal logical premises that necessarily lead to 

--- Page 61 ---

61
3.3. Critical Assessment of Theological Reasoning
the conclusion.” Whatever is meant by “formal logical premises,” it seems 
that the chatbot remained focused on the problem of analogy, even though 
this issue concerns the status of premises and not the formal correctness 
of the reasoning and its deductive nature. As ChatGPT claims, the reason­
ing “uses a metaphysical analogy (light, begetting splendor, and warmth) 
to help explain the relationship between the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.”
Gemini too stated that the reasoning “can be classified as reductive rea­
soning, specifically explanation.” It claimed that “its primary goal is to offer 
an explanation, rather than to prove or infer something about the Trinity.”
Both answers are entirely incorrect. The reasoning was not intended 
to give an explanation. This was not the intention of the author, but even 
without contextual knowledge, this intention cannot be observed in the 
structure of the reasoning. Grosseteste clearly attempted to construct the 
reasoning in a deductive manner. Even from the opening part of the rea­
soning (“The fact that God is a trinity of persons follows from the fact that 
God is light”) it is clear that Grosseteste wanted to prove God is a Trin­
ity, starting with the statement that God is light. This aim was completely 
overlooked by the chatbots. To sum up, they entirely failed to classify the 
reasoning accurately, once again exposing their limitations with respect to 
the analysis of the logical structure and validity of reasonings. On these 
grounds, the rating for criterion 3.3 should be reduced for both chatbots.
3.3.4. Soundness Assessment
In performing the tasks presented above, the chatbots referred to the 
soundness of the analysed theological argumentation. According to the 
standard definition, a reasoning is logically sound when it is valid and 
based on true premises. In this case, the chatbots were not explicitly asked 
to assess soundness (nor validity), but to evaluate the correctness of the 
reasoning. It was implicitly assumed that they would use both categories 
to evaluate correctness.
In T3.1, the chatbots were asked if St. Paul’s reasoning regarding God’s 
promise is correct. ChatGPT said: “As a Catholic theologian, I would 

--- Page 62 ---

62
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
affirm that Paul’s argument is theologically sound within the framework 
of Christian revelation. The Church teaches that God’s plan for salvation 
has always involved divine election and grace, not simply human effort or 
ethnic heritage.” This response shows that ChatGPT identified the sentence 
about God’s plan for salvation as the basic premise of St Paul’s argumenta­
tion and accepted this sentence as true according to Christian revelation. 
The quoted utterance also suggests that the chatbot found the Apostle’s 
reasoning valid. Indeed, in its answer ChatGPT indicates the main claim 
of the reasoning and explains how it is argued by St Paul using examples 
from the Old Testament. Thus, even if it was not explicitly stated, it seems 
that the chatbot found the reasoning valid. To sum up, it seems that it cor­
rectly assessed the soundness of the argument by taking into account both 
components: validity of the reasoning and verity of its premises.
Similarly, in the answer to T3.2, ChatGPT stated that “the overall rea­
soning is valid, supported by scriptural references, and flows logically from 
the premises to the conclusion.” Validity is explicitly mentioned in this 
case. It seems that the support of “scriptural references” guarantees the 
truth of the premises (as statements from the Bible should not be ques­
tioned within the theological framework). Thus, the chatbot concluded 
that the reasoning concerning Jabez was sound. However, one could argue 
that this judgment was based on an incorrect assessment of its validity, 
in light of the problems discussed in section 3.3.3. If that is the case, then 
the judgment concerning the soundness of the reasoning is also burdened 
with a lack of logical precision. Accordingly, the rating for criterion 3.4 
should be reduced.
Finally, in the answer to T3.6, ChatGPT stated that Grosseteste’s rea­
soning concerning the Trinity is “logically correct within the bounds of 
theological analogy and metaphysical argumentation.” In light of its previ­
ous remarks, it suggested that the reasoning was valid, though it acknowl­
edged that the premises could be questioned if one does not accept the 
analogical and metaphysical approach adopted in some of them. If so, then 
it can be said that the chatbot did, in fact, correctly assess the soundness 
of Grosseteste’s reasoning. However, in the last sentence of the answer, we 
read that it is “not a strict deductive proof of the Trinity.” If it is logically 

--- Page 63 ---

63
3.3. Critical Assessment of Theological Reasoning
correct, how can it be considered non-deductive? Probably, the chatbot as­
sumed that the analogical and metaphysical approaches not only informed 
the premises, but also shaped the reasoning itself. However, if that was the 
case, the chatbot’s assessment was misguided. In this instance, the ana­
logical and metaphysical components are embodied in the premises only, 
and the structure of the reasoning is logically valid.
To critique the reasoning effectively, one must instead scrutinise the 
premises, including the claim that “Every light has by nature and essence 
this characteristic, that it begets its splendour from itself.” Grosseteste 
seems to apply “every” universally, including to God, whom he under­
stands as true light—not just metaphorically, but as transcending bodily 
and non-bodily forms of light. This is precisely the moment at which one 
can disagree with him, arguing that to say that “every” light has such a 
characteristic lacks justification. It is, after all, a metaphysical assumption 
of Grosseteste’s framework. To sum up, it seems that ChatGPT made a 
mistake, and thus it struggled to assess the soundness of the reasoning. 
However, from its analyses we can conclude that the reasoning is not 
sound. In consequence, the chatbot was helpful.
In the answer to T3.1, Gemini did not use the category of soundness. It 
just stated that “from a Catholic theological perspective, St. Paul’s reason­
ing is considered correct.” It also did not analyse the reasoning to verify 
whether it is in fact correct. It only linked it to the theory of predestination, 
as accepted in Catholic theology. Thus, on these grounds, we cannot judge 
if Gemini meets criterion 3.4.
However, Gemini explicitly used the category of soundness in its an­
swer to T3.2, where it asserted that the reasoning concerning Jabez “is not 
logically sound.” The chatbot argued that the reasoning “contains errors in 
equivocation, non-sequitur, begging the question, and false analogy.” This 
shows that the chatbot focused on showing that it was not valid, as it did not 
refer to the truth of the premises. Perhaps there was no reason to question 
the premises, as they were based on passages from the Bible (which should 
not be questioned within the theological framework), or the meaning of the 
name Jabez. It seems that Gemini’s criticism is right. Hence, this confirms 
that the chatbot can assess the soundness of theological argumentation.

--- Page 64 ---

64
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
Gemini also explicitly used the category of soundness in the answer to 
T3.6, where it stated that Grosseteste’s reasoning “is not logically sound 
or conclusive” and pointed out “the use of analogy, assumptions, and po­
tential circular reasoning.” Although, as previously noted, Gemini’s inter­
pretation was to some extent mistaken, the use of analogy does indeed 
raise legitimate questions about Grosseteste’s reasoning. When assessing 
the soundness of the reasoning, the chatbot was justified in scrutinising 
certain assumptions. Insofar as it disagreed with some of them, it is also 
entitled to evaluate the reasoning as unsound. In sum, while some parts 
of its assessment are controversial, Gemini once again demonstrated its 
capacity to analyse reasoning in terms of soundness.
Interestingly, it seems that in the pilot testing, Gemini’s predecessor, 
Bard, gave more accurate answers to tasks similar to T3.2 and T3.6. In the 
assessment of the reasoning used in T3.2, Bard judged that “the reasoning 
is logically valid, but its correctness depends on the truth of the premises.” 
This means that in Bard’s view it was valid, but not necessarily sound. Bard 
indicated that “the premises in this argument are based on the interpre­
tation of biblical passages” and “the interpretation of these passages is a 
matter of debate, and there is no consensus on their meaning.” For the 
reasoning used in T3.6, Bard listed the strengths and weaknesses of the 
reasoning. Among the strengths, it mentioned the fact that “the reasoning 
is logically sound, and it does not make any obvious mistakes.” Although 
it appears that the chatbot should have said “valid” instead of “sound,” its 
intended message was that the reasoning is formally correct. Among the 
weaknesses, we find the following: “The reasoning is based on a number of 
obscure and controversial claims, such as the claim that every light has by 
nature and essence the characteristic of begetting its splendor from itself.” 
This means that the premises raise legitimate doubts. Although in the case 
of the reasoning from T3.2, one can object to its validity, as it requires ad­
ditional premises linking together the existing ones for correct deduction, 
Bard rightly identified the key problem, which is the acceptance of the in­
terpretation of biblical sentences. Moreover, the assessment of soundness 
of the reasoning from T3.6 seems to be right.

--- Page 65 ---

65
3.4. Formulating Theological Reasoning
In T3.8, both chatbots had an opportunity to expand on the soundness 
of reasonings, as they were asked to identify traces of non-classical logic. 
However, both chatbots focused on exploring the topic of non-classical 
approaches in logic and did not evaluate the presented reasonings.
Finally, ChatGPT was directly asked to check the soundness of a rea­
soning in T5.5. This time, the task concerned a reasoning it had produced 
itself. The chatbot performed the task relatively well, though with less criti­
cal rigour compared to its evaluations in the previous examples.
To sum up, both chatbots are capable of assessing the soundness of rea­
soning, and they are useful in indicating potential issues in this respect. 
However, their answers are not without flaws. They sometimes misidentify 
problems or introduce confusion. In fact, it seems that an earlier chatbot 
developed by Google (Bard) offered a better response to at least one similar 
task. Thus, they should not receive a high rating for criterion 3.4.
3.4. Formulating Theological Reasoning
3.4.1. Drawing Conclusions from Biblical Passages
In T4.1, the chatbots were asked what conclusions they could draw as 
Christian theologians from the statement “I am in the Father and the Fa­
ther is in me,” assuming that it was uttered by Jesus Christ.
ChatGPT listed seven conclusions; however, it presented them as sec­
tions with one or two paragraphs of discussion. Let us extract the key 
statement from each section, which may serve as its proper conclusion: 
1. “The statement emphasizes the unity of essence between God the Father 
and God the Son.” 2. “The phrase highlights the relationality within the 
Trinity. (…) The mutual indwelling—called perichoresis or circuminces­
sion—describes how the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit interpenetrate each 
other while remaining distinct persons.” 3. “Jesus’ statement suggests that 
He is the perfect revelation of the Father.” 4. “The statement also implies 
that Jesus’ mission is not independent but fully united with the Father’s 
will.” 5. “The mutual indwelling also points to the mystery of the Incar­

--- Page 66 ---

66
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
nation, where the divine Son takes on human nature.” 6. “The union be­
tween Father and Son invites believers to enter into that same commu­
nion.” 7. “Since Jesus is in the Father and the Father is in Him, He serves 
as the bridge through which divine life and salvation are communicated to 
the world.” The task was later repeated in a separate conversation to check 
if the quality of the answer changed significantly. The new answer had a 
similar structure with six sections. Here is the extract: 1. There is “the pro­
found unity between Jesus (the Son) and God the Father, which is central 
to the doctrine of the Trinity.” The sameness of divine essence and peri­
choresis (mutual indwelling) were also mentioned in this section. 2. “Je­
sus is fully God, possessing all the attributes of God the Father, including 
omnipotence, omniscience, and eternality” and “even in His humanity, 
Jesus remains united with the Father, affirming the coexistence of His two 
natures—divine and human.” 3. “Jesus is the perfect and complete revela­
tion of God.” 4. There is “the love, communication, and relational unity 
between the Father and the Son.” 5. Jesus’ “unity with the Father makes it 
possible for human beings to be united with God.” 6. “Jesus’ words are not 
His alone but are directly from the Father.”
Gemini gave a shorter answer. However, it still requires extracting the 
key conclusions to make it more concise: 1. There is “the essential unity 
between the Son and the Father, while also acknowledging their distinct 
personalities.” 2. “Jesus’ divine nature is fully rooted in and inseparable 
from God, and that God’s presence is fully manifested in Jesus.” 3. “Jesus 
asserts his divine authority and identity as the Son of God.” 4. “The divine 
nature of God is fully present within the human nature of Jesus.” 5. “Jesus’ 
mission and authority ultimately derive from his divine nature and his 
intimate relationship with God.”
The conclusions given by both chatbots are correct from the point of 
view of Christian theology. They also cover the most important theo­
logical content indeed related to that utterance. In this part, the task was 
performed competently by both chatbots. However, they did not attempt 
to construct strictly logical reasoning for each of the conclusions. To ad­
dress this, they were presented with a follow-up task (T4.1*), in which they 
were prompted to formulate a reasoning based on the biblical statement 

--- Page 67 ---

67
3.4. Formulating Theological Reasoning
“I am in the Father and the Father is in me” which would lead to one of the 
chatbots’ own conclusions: for ChatGPT—that Christ is God; for Gemini: 
conclusion 2.
ChatGPT initially formulated an overly extensive reply that did not 
constitute proper reasoning. When prompted again to present it as a proof, 
it continued to offer a lengthy, multi-step explanation. The last prompt 
(“Please present it concisely, in formal logical style”) proved effective. This 
time, the chatbot listed six premises followed by six subsequent conclu­
sions, indicating how each was derived from the preceding statements. 
The final conclusion was “Jesus Christ is God, sharing the same divine 
essence as the Father.” At first glance, the proof appears to be correct. 
However, it contains certain logical gaps, for instance, inferring C3 from 
C2 and P4 seems unwarranted. Moreover, the proof includes redundant 
content, such as P6. Also, it was unnecessary to include the topic of Je­
sus’s divine attributes to prove his divinity based on the selected biblical 
passage, so the proof could be simplified. In the repeated testing, in the 
final answer, the set of premises was better—they were sufficient on their 
own to support the intended conclusion. However, this time ChatGPT 
did not show precisely what was drawn from what and just stated: “From 
Premises 1–6, it follows that Jesus possesses the same divine essence as 
God the Father, thereby confirming His divine identity. Therefore, Jesus 
is not merely a prophet or human, but truly God, co-equal and co-eternal 
with the Father.”
Gemini started with a shorter answer, but it also did not have the form 
of a clear reasoning, so it was asked to “present it as a logical proof,” where 
all necessary premises and conclusions are listed and where it is shown 
from which premises the conclusions are drawn. It was expected to cre­
ate a reasoning leading to conclusion 2, that is, the one related to divine 
intimacy. However, Gemini produced a reasoning leading to a conclusion 
about the Trinity. The reasoning was well constructed. However, when the 
chatbot was instructed to present this reasoning as a logical proof (like 
ChatGPT), it failed entirely. While it did list the premises and subsequent 
conclusions, showing what is derived from what, the output was riddled 
with mistakes. Second, the last step was not based on logical connections, 

--- Page 68 ---

68
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
and offered no sufficient grounds for the final conclusion. Third, the rea­
soning began with Premise 1: “Jesus is a divine person,” a clear instance of 
begging the question.
In T4.2, the chatbots were asked to consider the relation of being in: 
In(x, y), to explain what it means for x to be in y, and to prove, using the 
statement “I am in the Father and the Father is in me” (uttered by Jesus as 
the Son), that the Son and the Father have the same essence.
ChatGPT added a formal symbol for the essence of x: E(x), and intro­
duced the premise: In(x, y)  ⟹  E(x) = E(y). The chatbot based it on the 
definition of the relation In(x, y), which, according to ChatGPT, implies 
the identity of essence of the two arguments of this relation. However, the 
chatbot failed to acknowledge that generally “being in” does not entail 
such identity. This holds only in special situations, such as when In(x, y) 
and In(y, x). Hence the premise should be modified to the following form: 
“In(x, y) ∧ In(y, x)  ⟹  E(x) = E(y).” The proof is not very elegant, but it 
would be plausible were it not for this one mistake. That said, a logically 
correct proof can be constructed with such a premise. The only problem is 
that the premise is questionable.
Gemini first created a reasoning in natural language (which was not 
even formulated in the form of a syllogism), so it was instructed to provide 
a formal proof.
It almost succeeded. It introduced an axiom: ∀x∀y(In(x, y) ∧ In(y, x) → 
E(x, y)), which was a better choice compared to ChatGPT. It linked it with 
Jesus’s words in the following way: “In(P, F) ∧ In(F, P).” From these two 
statements it deduced that E(P, F). It would have been an adequate proof 
were it not for the fact that Gemini first introduced axiom 1, which was 
both logically redundant and empirically implausible. This axiom, pre­
sented by the chatbot in a formal way as “∀x∀y(In(x, y) → In(y, x)),” pur­
portedly captured “the idea that the relation ‘In’ is symmetrical.” This mir­
rors the mistake made by ChatGPT. The only difference is that Gemini did 
not use it in the proof.
In summary, the chatbots can produce theological conclusions based 
on biblical passages, but they struggle to construct logically correct argu­
ments that support those conclusions. Their output often includes mistakes 

--- Page 69 ---

69
3.4. Formulating Theological Reasoning
and redundant content. They have the potential to use formal logic in such 
tasks, and it enhances the quality of their reasoning. However, even if they 
perform the task better when adopting the formal approach, they still pro­
duce redundant content, some of which contradicts common experience. 
For these reasons, a high rating for criterion 4.1 would not be warranted.
3.4.2. Drawing Conclusions from Theological Statements
To verify whether the chatbots meet criterion 4.2, they were assigned T4.3: 
“Assume you are a Catholic theologian applying logic strictly. What key 
conclusions can be drawn from the statement ‘God is good’? Provide the 
additional premises necessary to support your conclusions.”
ChatGPT presented the following conclusion: “God’s goodness is iden­
tical to His essence.” This conclusion is deductible within Catholic the­
ology. It can be derived by adding two premises, which are commonly 
accepted in Catholic theology: (1) every divine attribute is identical with 
God’s essence, (2) goodness is one of God’s attributes.
However, ChatGPT followed a more convoluted and ultimately incor­
rect line of reasoning. It relied on the theological statement that God’s ex­
istence is identical with God’s essence and a metaphysical statement popu­
lar in 13th-century Catholic theology: that goodness is convertible with 
being. Yet convertibility does not entail identity. Thus, ChatGPT’s attempt 
was a failure. The reasoning could be fixed by replacing this premise with 
another one: God’s goodness is identical with God’s existence. However, 
this statement was absent in the chatbot’s reasoning. Finally, ChatGPT at­
tempted to present the reasoning using symbolic logic and showing from 
which statements the subsequent conclusions are drawn. Unfortunately, 
conclusion C3 is not derivable from premises P2 and P3, as the chatbot 
claims. Moreover, it is peculiar that despite (mistakenly) treating C3 as 
the final conclusion (i.e., God’s goodness is identical with God’s essence), 
the chatbot proceeded to formulate yet another conclusion (C4). Finally, 
no conclusion was drawn from P5 (which seems crucial to the reasoning, 
as it is a premise stating that goodness and being are convertible). Thus, 

--- Page 70 ---

70
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
although the chatbot indicated a conclusion which can be indeed drawn 
by a Catholic theologian from the sentence “God is good,” in the second 
part, the task was poorly-performed and chaotic, and it included redun­
dant content. Consequently, ChatGPT cannot be awarded a high rating for 
this criterion.
Gemini proposed a different conclusion, namely “God is morally per­
fect.” It added two premises to the starting one (Premise 1: “God is good”), 
namely, Premise 2: “If something is good, it possesses the highest degree 
of moral virtue,” and Premise 3: “God, as the ultimate being, possesses 
all morally perfect qualities.” Next it presented a reasoning according to 
which “From Premise 1 and Premise 2, we can conclude that God possess­
es the highest degree of moral virtue”; and in the next step: “Since moral 
perfection is the highest degree of moral virtue, we can infer that God is 
morally perfect.” This reasoning contains a flaw: Premise 3 is not actu­
ally used in the reasoning. Moreover, the reasoning suffers from a lack­
ing premise—one that would equate the highest degree of moral virtue 
with moral perfection. In order to salvage the reasoning, Premise 3 should 
be replaced with the following: “Moral perfection is the highest degree of 
moral virtue.” Although the correct conclusion was ultimately reached, 
and the line of reasoning required to arrive at it was clarified, the chat­
bot’s argumentative process contained significant flaws. Hence, it cannot 
receive a maximum rating for criterion 4.2.
3.4.3. Giving Reasons for Theological Statements
To check whether the chatbots can effectively provide reasons for theologi­
cal statements, they were given T4.4: “Assume you are a Catholic theolo­
gian. Can you offer a strict proof that God is Trinity?”
ChatGPT correctly defined key terms essential for the reasoning and 
listed premises supporting them with passages from the Bible. On these 
grounds, it carried out a correct proof leading to the final conclusion “God 
is Trinity: one divine essence in three distinct persons—Father, Son, and 
Holy Spirit.” In fact, this conclusion had already been reached in the fourth 

--- Page 71 ---

71
3.4. Formulating Theological Reasoning
step (conclusion C4). It seems that adding C5 was unnecessary, especially 
since it introduces the idea that divine persons are distinct in their rela­
tions, which is not rooted in the premises. This part of the reasoning thus 
constitutes redundant content. Besides this, the reasoning is correct, and 
in each step ChatGPT clearly showed from which statements a new conclu­
sion was drawn. The task was therefore performed well: The chatbot found 
sufficient reasons for the theological statement and conducted a strict and 
correct proof.
Gemini’s reaction was unexpected—instead of producing a proof, it in­
terpreted the question as an inquiry about its capabilities. This is akin to 
asking someone “Can you open the window?,” and receiving a reply such 
as “Yes, I can. I would approach the window, turn the handle, and pull the 
sash,” without actually opening it. This means that the lack of a conven­
tional courtesy phrase, such as “Would you please,” appears to have caused 
a misreading of the intention of the task, which was to request the genera­
tion of a proof. In its response, Gemini first made some caveats concerning 
theological proofs, which are different from the mathematical ones. Sec­
ond, it stated: “we can present a logical argument based on key theological 
principles and scriptural passages to support the Trinity,” and it listed the 
elements that “this argument would involve.” The response included all 
the premises needed to construct the proof, so there was no need to ask the 
chatbot to do that, as the answer sufficiently demonstrated Gemini could 
perform the task. At the same time, this was an interesting case showing 
how much prompting can influence the outcome.
T4.5 was similar to T4.4, but formulated differently: “Assume you are a 
logically thinking Catholic theologian. Is it possible to claim that hell will 
be empty? If so, what reasons can you give in support of this view?”
ChatGPT presented the key premises relevant for the debate on an 
empty hell within the framework of Catholic theology. It identified which 
premises support the conclusion that hell may be empty, and it also in­
dicated what additional conclusion can be drawn from other premises. 
Finally, it referred to the teaching of the Catholic Church and stated: “It 
is not possible to definitively claim that hell will be empty, as this would 
contradict the Church’s teaching on free will, divine justice, and the real 

--- Page 72 ---

72
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
possibility of eternal separation from God. However, it is possible to hope 
that all will be saved and that hell may be empty, based on God’s infinite 
mercy and the universal offer of salvation through Christ.” To sum up, 
ChatGPT performed the task effectively. It gave reasons for the claim in 
question, and precisely argued for its final conclusion.
Gemini adopted a more restrictive stance, stating: “As a Catholic theo­
logian, I cannot logically claim that Hell will be empty.” Consequently, it 
did not give any reasons for such a claim. On the contrary, it listed “some 
key reasons why the Catholic Church maintains that Hell will not be emp­
ty.” They included information about eternal fire and eternal punishment 
from the Gospel of Matthew 25, the idea of God’s just judgment according 
to people’s actions, and human free will and the possibility to choose evil 
and reject God’s mercy. Gemini correctly identified the principles which 
make the claim about an empty hell problematic. However, at the same 
time it showed it is not flexible enough to creatively verify whether there is 
any way of reconciling those principles with the claim or at least hope that 
hell might be empty. This seems to be a limitation, because even if Gemini 
rejects the possibility of expressing such a claim within the framework 
of Catholic theology, it would still have been instructive for the chatbot 
to present some of the reasons offered by those who express such a claim 
or hope, like ChatGPT did. Again, it seems that the chatbot did not un­
derstand the intention of the question. It treated the question “If so, what 
reasons can you give in support of this view?” as conditional on its own 
acceptance of the claim, rather than as a prompt to explore potential argu­
ments regardless of theological commitment.
To sum up, Gemini demonstrated that it was capable of giving reasons 
for theological statements, since it did so, although for the opposite view. 
At the same time, it showed that it can be overly strict, and, in conse­
quence, less useful than it could otherwise be. However, there is no doubt 
that different, perhaps more direct prompting would have eventually led it 
to provide a list of reasons for the claim that hell is empty. Overall, Gemini 
fulfilled the task competently.

--- Page 73 ---

73
3.5. Linking Biblical Utterances to Theological Statements
3.5. Linking Biblical Utterances to Theological 
Statements
3.5.1. Constructing a Reasoning
Criterion 5.1 was designed to assess whether chatbots are able to construct 
reasonings which combine biblical utterances with theological statements. 
For this criterion, T5.1, T5.2, and additionally T4.2 were used. T5.1 was 
formulated as follows: “Can you construct a deductive reasoning based 
on the premise ‘God is light’ that arrives at the conclusion ‘God is a trin­
ity of persons’? Be creative, but remain logically strict, without short­
cuts.” This task was of course inspired by the reasoning created by Robert 
Grosseteste, which was used in T3.6. It proved important to include the 
phrase “Be creative” in the prompt, as pilot testing revealed that, without this 
encouragement, some chatbots refused to perform the task, claiming it was 
impossible. However, once prompted to be creative, they accepted the 
challenge.
ChatGPT offered an extensive answer; however, in the end it presented 
the following concise reasoning:
Premise: God is light.
Light has a unified yet dual nature, which suggests that God’s 
nature is similarly unified yet manifold.
Light emanates, illuminates, and interacts with creation, which 
corresponds to the roles of Father (source), Son (emanation), and 
Holy Spirit (interaction).
The unified nature of light (despite its distinct properties) mirrors 
the unity of God, even in the distinction of persons.
Conclusion: God is a trinity of persons.
Finally, the chatbot added: “Each step logically follows from both the 
nature of light and traditional theological concepts, allowing us to conclude 
that if God is light, then God must also be a trinity of persons.” This means 

--- Page 74 ---

74
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
that the chatbot did not see any problems with the reasoning, including the 
potential fallacy of begging the question in the penultimate step. Another 
issue is that it did not indicate clearly which sentences were the premises and 
which of them were intermediate conclusions, as well as which sentences 
were drawn from which previously accepted statements. However, the rea­
soning is indeed creative, and the chatbot successfully found a way to logi­
cally link the two statements. In the next prompt, it was asked whether it had 
encountered this line of reasoning in existing sources, or had developed it 
independently. ChatGPT replied: “I invented this reasoning by myself based 
on theological principles and analogies drawn from the nature of light.” 
Internet searches did not yield any instances of this particular reasoning, so 
it is quite probable that the model indeed invented the reasoning by itself.
Gemini proposed the following reasoning:
Premise 1:	
God is light.
Premise 2:	
Light is a phenomenon that exhibits a tripartite 
nature, consisting of:
—	Intensity: The measure of light’s energy or brightness.
—	Frequency: The color or wavelength of light.
—	Direction: The path or vector of light’s propagation.
Premise 3:	
God, being a perfect and complete being, must fully 
manifest all aspects of his nature.
Inference 1:	 Since God is light, he must exhibit the tripartite 
nature of light.
Inference 2:	 The intensity of light can be interpreted as the Father, 
the source of all creation.
Inference 3:	 The frequency of light can be interpreted as the Son, 
the manifestation of God’s love and truth.
Inference 4:	 The direction of light can be interpreted as the Holy 
Spirit, the active force of God’s presence and power.
Conclusion:	 Therefore, God, being light, is a trinity of persons: the 
Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit.

--- Page 75 ---

75
3.5. Linking Biblical Utterances to Theological Statements
In the end, Gemini added a note to highlight that this reasoning “is a 
creative and speculative interpretation of the biblical concept of God as 
light,” that “this is not a traditional or universally accepted theological proof 
of the Trinity,” and that “the concept of the Trinity is primarily grounded 
in scripture and theological tradition.” As we can see, Gemini did not claim 
that the reasoning was logically correct. However, its quality is quite similar 
to the one presented by ChatGPT. Gemini did not mark the logical connec­
tions between its elements. However, the reasoning is undeniably creative. 
Gemini also confirmed that it had invented the reasoning by itself.
Both chatbots performed the task well. Even if the reasonings they pre­
sented should be complemented with additional information, they con­
structed reasonings which combined two given statements in a plausible way.
T5.2 was similar to T5.1. The chatbots were asked whether they can 
construct a deductive reasoning which is based on the premise “I am who 
I am” and has as a conclusion “God exists.”
Once again, ChatGPT provided an extensive and elaborate answer, but 
in the end it presented the following “Summary of Deductive Reasoning”:
Premise: “I am who I am” implies absolute self-existence and 
necessary existence.
A self-existent being cannot not exist, as its essence includes 
existence.
Contingent beings require the existence of a necessary being, which 
must be self-existent.
The self-existent necessary being is what we refer to as God.
Conclusion: Therefore, God exists.
The structure of the reasoning is similar to the response to T5.1, and 
also in this case, the information provided about the logical connections is 
insufficient. However, the idea is clear. It seems that ChatGPT succeeded 
in creating a reasoning which links together the two statements. Interest­
ingly, it did not attempt to take a shortcut and add that, according to the 
Bible, “I am who I am” was uttered by God. It took up the challenge of con­

--- Page 76 ---

76
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
structing its reasoning only on the basis of the utterance being accepted 
as true. Drawing on the concepts of self-existence and necessary being, it 
proceeded to demonstrate that these attributes can apply only to God, ulti­
mately concluding that God exists. What is more, it emphasised the logical 
validity of the produced reasoning, asserting that it is “tightly structured, 
relying on the logical implications of self-existence and necessary exist­
ence to conclude that God, as the necessary being, must exist.”
Interestingly, Gemini followed a similar strategy, basing its reasoning 
on the concept of self-existence and its necessity (but this time without the 
concept of necessary being), and successfully led to the conclusion “God 
exists.” In the end, it added that this reasoning “is known as the onto­
logical argument for God’s existence, first proposed by Anselm of Can­
terbury,” which is not true, because this argument does not rely on the 
formula “I am who I am.” However, the reasoning presented by the chatbot 
does contain some elements characteristic of the ontological argument.
To sum up, both chatbots performed the task quite well.
Finally, as previously discussed, they also demonstrated similar com­
petences when performing T4.2. Although they made some mistakes in 
the reasonings presented in their answers, they plausibly bridged the two 
given statements.
While these imperfections prevent them from receiving the maximum 
rating for criterion 5.1, they deserve a relatively high score overall.
3.5.2. Objecting to Linking the Statements
Next, it was important to check whether, after those successful creative 
productions, the chatbots can exercise restraint and acknowledge that some 
statements cannot be logically linked. In T5.3, they were asked whether 
they could construct a deductive reasoning based on the premise “I am who 
I am,” with the conclusion “God gave free will to human beings.” Of course, 
from a formal point of view, it is always possible to connect two consistent 
statements by adding a premise which joins them in the form of an implica­
tion. However, when we take into account the content of the statements, it 

--- Page 77 ---

77
3.5. Linking Biblical Utterances to Theological Statements
may sometimes be too difficult to find a reasonable bridge between them. 
That seemed to be the case with the pair of sentences used in T5.3.
But not for ChatGPT and Gemini, which followed the pattern of the 
previous answers, each producing a reasoning bridging the two sentences 
(additionally, ChatGPT claimed that the reasoning was deductive). Both 
chatbots decided to interpret the biblical sentence in a way that enabled 
them to easily reach the conclusion. ChatGPT started with the premise 
that “I am who I am” expresses God’s absolute freedom and self-deter­
mination, whereas Gemini claimed that the statement “I am who I am” 
implies a being of infinite wisdom and goodness. Such bridges are difficult 
to accept, and it is likely that, if the chatbots were asked to evaluate this 
material, they would identify those sentences as weak points.
Next, the chatbots were assigned similar tasks: T5.4 and T5.4* with new 
pairs of statements, this time purely philosophical ones. Bridging these 
statements would require committing the so-called naturalistic fallacy 
(i.e., deriving an “ought” from an “is”). In T5.4, the premise was “We are 
human beings,” and the conclusion: “We should be happy”; in T5.4*, re­
spectively, “We exist” and “We should be protected by other beings.” The 
results were similar to those obtained in the answers to T5.3. The chatbots 
did not object and did not recognise the problem of the naturalistic fallacy.
This kind of testing suggests that if we encourage the chatbots to be cre­
ative, and if they are provided with a warmup in bridging statements, chat­
bots tend to perform the task—even when the statements are conceptually 
distant. This means that in such situations they are willing to suspend their 
criticism, which is very important for a rational theologian.
3.5.3. Discussing Possible Connections
It is also important to evaluate the connection between a biblical utter­
ance and a given theological statement. This involves, first, identifying the 
connection; second: adopting a certain interpretative framework that en-
ables such a connection (e.g., a specific theological perspective); and third: 
showing the weak points of such an approach.

--- Page 78 ---

78
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
In T5.5, each chatbot was asked to go back to the reasoning it had pro­
duced in the answer to T5.3 (bridging “I am who I am” and “God gave 
free will to human beings”). ChatGPT was instructed to verify whether the 
reasoning was sound. Gemini was assigned a more direct task: “Are there 
interpretations that link the above-mentioned pairs of statements? If so, 
can you identify their weaknesses?”
ChatGPT started with defining soundness: “A sound argument is one 
that is both valid (the logical structure is correct, and the conclusion fol­
lows from the premises) and has true premises.” Next, it presented the rea­
soning again, and judged that it is logically valid. Finally, it examined the 
premises individually to determine if they are true, providing a sentence 
labelled as “verdict” for each, which included an explanation of the frame­
work within which a given premise is true. For instance, according  to the 
chatbot, the premise asserting that “I am who I am” expresses God’s ab­
solute freedom and self-determination “is generally accepted within many 
theological frameworks” and “has traditionally been interpreted as point­
ing to God’s self-existence, independence, and sovereign freedom.” This 
led to the following verdict: “Likely true within the context of many reli­
gious and philosophical traditions.” Having examined all the premises in 
a similar manner, the chatbot concluded that “the argument is valid,” “the 
conclusion logically follows from the premises,” “the premises are all true 
or likely true within the context of many theological and philosophical 
frameworks, particularly those based on Judeo-Christian principles,” and 
formulated the final verdict: “The argument is sound within the context of 
a theological framework that accepts: God’s freedom and self-determina­
tion, humans being made in God’s image; freedom entailing free will, and 
the purpose of free will being to enable a genuine relationship with God.” 
However, in the end, it emphasised that “it would not be sound for those 
who do not share these underlying theological or philosophical assump­
tions.” It seems that the chatbot, while examining the soundness of the 
reasoning, also analysed the connection between the two sentences, iden­
tified the framework within which the premises can be accepted as true, 
and finally indicated a weak point, which is that not everybody is willing 
to adopt the listed assumptions.

--- Page 79 ---

79
3.6. Discussing Issues in Natural Theology
Gemini presented three interpretations that were deemed necessary to 
establish the connection between the two sentences in question. Each was 
formulated as a brief argument. Thus, strictly speaking, they were not in­
terpretations in the sense of special readings within a specific framework, 
but rather forms of argumentation. Let us quote the first pair as an example:
Interpretation 1: God’s Self-Existence and Human Free Will
Argument: Since God is self-existent, he is the ultimate source of all 
existence. As the source of human existence, he has the authority to 
grant humans free will.
Weakness: This interpretation assumes that God’s self-existence 
automatically grants him the authority to control the nature of 
his creations. It doesn’t address the question of why God would 
choose to grant free will, or whether there could be other possible 
outcomes of his self-existence.
We can see that such a weakness reveals a potential shortcut or a hid­
den assumption. This was also the case in subsequent interpretations. As 
Gemini stated in the concluding sentence, its “interpretations” “ultimately 
rely on assumptions about God’s nature, intentions, and the nature of hu­
man existence.”
It seems that both chatbots performed the task well by identifying: con­
nections between the statements in question and interpretations necessary 
to establish such connections, as well as weak points of these interpretations.
3.6. Discussing Issues in Natural Theology
3.6.1. Constructing Argumentation within Natural Theology
To check whether the chatbots can construct argumentation for a theo­
logical claim not based on revelation, T6.1 and T6.1* were used. In the first 
task, they were asked to present a rational argument supporting the claim 
that human beings have life after death. The second task was similar, but 

--- Page 80 ---

80
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
concerned the claim that God exists. In both tasks, the instruction was: “If 
there are many arguments, give the strongest one.”
ChatGPT presented extensively the subsequent steps of the argumenta­
tion for the possibility of an afterlife. It highlighted that evidence for that 
claim is insufficient; however, “the argument from consciousness and the 
irreducibility of subjective experience suggests that if consciousness is not 
fully reducible to physical brain processes, then it is rationally plausible to 
believe that it could survive the death of the body.” The chatbot judged that 
it “provides a strong, rational basis for believing that life may continue in 
some form beyond physical death.” This means that the chatbot sought to 
adopt a moderate stance, choosing not to present arguments which would 
not be strong enough, such as, for example, one of Plato’s arguments from 
the Phaedo, or St Augustine’s proof from the Soliloquies. However, it seems it 
would have been instructive for the chatbot to at least mention such attempts.
For T6.1*, ChatGPT extensively and correctly discussed the Kalam 
cosmological argument, which “provides a rational and intuitive line of 
reasoning leading to the conclusion that the universe has a cause, and this 
cause has the attributes traditionally associated with God.” In the chatbot’s 
view, “it provides one of the strongest rational arguments for the existence 
of God.” Interestingly, ChatGPT did not include any caveat to warn that it 
is possible to refute the argument.
In the answer to T6.1, Gemini started with a disclaimer that “it’s im­
portant to note that the existence of life after death is a matter of belief and 
faith for many” and “there is no scientific consensus or definitive proof to 
support the claim.” Next, it gave a similar solution to the one provided by 
ChatGPT. It presented an argument based on the idea that “If conscious­
ness is non-physical, it’s possible that it could exist independently of the 
physical body,” and next it briefly signalled other arguments that can be 
taken into account.
Also for T6.1*, Gemini offered a similar answer. It stated that “one of 
the most well-known philosophical arguments for God’s existence is the 
cosmological argument.” It presented the key steps of the argument and its 
strengths, as well as possible criticism. However, upon closer analysis, the 

--- Page 81 ---

81
3.6. Discussing Issues in Natural Theology
selection of problems mentioned seems somewhat peculiar. This will be 
discussed in the following section.
Generally, both chatbots performed the task well and gave instructive 
answers. For the argument concerning the afterlife, they highlighted that 
it only supports the possibility of life after death. However, no classical ar­
gument based on specific metaphysical assumptions, like Plato’s proofs for 
the immortality of the soul, was presented. They also provided one of the 
most common arguments for the existence of God. Notably, while Gemini 
included the above-mentioned criticism, ChatGPT made no reference to 
it. Overall, both chatbots deserve a relatively high rating for criterion 6.1.
3.6.2. Assessing Argumentation within Natural Theology
In T6.2, ChatGPT was asked to assess the argumentation it had provided. 
The chatbot presented the strengths and weaknesses of the Kalam argu­
ment. Among strengths, it included logical consistency and formulated 
the argumentation in the form of a syllogism: “Premise 1: Everything that 
begins to exist has a cause. Premise 2: The universe began to exist. Conclu­
sion: Therefore, the universe has a cause.” As it stated: “This logical struc­
ture is solid and hard to refute directly without challenging the premises.” 
The chatbot cited support from modern cosmology, claiming that “the idea 
that the universe began to exist aligns well with the Big Bang theory, which 
is the dominant scientific explanation for the origin of the universe,” and 
that “the notion of a finite beginning is supported by the expansion of 
the universe and the second law of thermodynamics (entropy).” The chat­
bot also enumerated other strengths, such as: addressing the fundamental 
question of existence, the fact that the argument makes minimal assump­
tions, and the reasonable next step in which the attributes of the cause of 
the universe that are being derived (timeless, spaceless, immaterial, pow­
erful, and personal) are at the same time “aligned with classical theism, 
making it a strong candidate for identifying the cause with God, rather 
than some impersonal force.” Next, ChatGPT extensively discussed the 
weaknesses. However, each weakness was followed by a response to show 

--- Page 82 ---

82
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
the possible defence of the argument. The chatbot included a challenge 
to each of the premises and to the conclusion of the syllogism presented 
above. It mentioned: that, according to quantum mechanics, it is possible 
that some quantum events occur without specific causes; that there are 
cosmological models which work without the beginning of the universe; 
that the argument does not convince us that the cause of the universe is 
necessarily a personal, intelligent being. The chatbot also pointed out other 
possible objections related to the nature of time as something that does not 
exist before the universe, and to the possibility of an infinite past. In each 
response, it demonstrated that the criticism either involved limitations, 
was inapplicable to the argument, or stemmed from a misunderstanding, 
ultimately failing to undermine the argument. In conclusion, the chatbot 
acknowledged that the argument must face objections, but maintained 
that it remains a compelling one.
Gemini was not asked to perform T6.2, because it provided an assess­
ment of the argumentation in its answer to T6.1. The chatbot listed the 
following strengths: “Simplicity: The cosmological argument is relatively 
straightforward and easy to understand. Logical consistency: The argu­
ment follows a logical structure, moving from premises to a conclusion. 
Relevance to the question of existence: It directly addresses the question of 
the universe’s origin and the possibility of a higher power.” Next Gemini 
presented the “criticisms of the argument,” including the following prob­
lems: “Infinite regress: Some argue that the argument could lead to an infi­
nite regress of causes, where each cause itself needs a cause. Scientific limi­
tations: The premise that the universe has a beginning is based on current 
scientific understanding, which could change in the future. Lack of empir­
ical evidence: The argument relies on philosophical reasoning rather than 
empirical observation, making it difficult to prove or disprove definitively.”
It appears that the first issue raised by Gemini does not, in fact, chal­
lenge the argument. On the contrary, the argument, at least in some ver­
sions, assumes the necessity of a first cause in order to avoid infinite re­
gress. When this was pointed out in the next prompt, Gemini acknowl­
edged its mistake. Next, undermining scientific knowledge on the grounds 
that it may change in the future is also highly questionable. While it is 

--- Page 83 ---

83
3.6. Discussing Issues in Natural Theology
true that science evolves, we have no better choice, and the best we can 
do is to trust science. Otherwise, we risk slipping into scepticism. The last 
objection is also problematic. It is natural that an argument referring to 
something beyond observable things (a timeless, spaceless, and immate­
rial God) must be a philosophical one, and that it will not be based on 
direct observation. Nonetheless, this argument is relatively strongly rooted 
in empirical knowledge. This is not to defend the argument, but rather 
to show that Gemini had chosen disputable or misplaced and, in conse­
quence, extremely weak objections, and failed to recognise these issues 
within its own reasoning.
Generally, both chatbots showed that they can assess argumentation 
within the framework of natural theology. However, the content provided 
by Gemini with respect to the potential weaknesses lacked sufficient rig­
our. For this reason, it should receive a lower rating than ChatGPT for 
criterion 6.2.
3.6.3. Limitations of Natural Theology
To check whether the chatbots can define the limitations of natural theolo­
gy, they were asked the following questions: “What theological statements 
can be rationally accepted? What theological arguments can be rationally 
justified. Why?” The omission of direct questions about such limitations 
was intentional. The aim was to observe whether they would mention these 
issues when asked about the rationality of theological statements and their 
justification.
ChatGPT presented eight theological statements and provided a rational 
justification for each. It also characterised the various types of justifications, 
showing when they rely on philosophical plausibility, when they draw on 
empirical support, etc. In the conclusion, the chatbot stated about the argu­
ments for such claims what follows: “While not universally accepted, they 
offer strong rational frameworks for those who seek to justify theological 
beliefs within the context of reason.” In this light, it was not clear whether 
according to ChatGPT natural theology does in fact have any serious limita­

--- Page 84 ---

84
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
tions; this was only suggested when the chatbot described the arguments as 
“not universally accepted.” Hence, ChatGPT was asked: “So should rational 
atheists accept these claims?” ChatGPT produced an extensive yet very ac­
curate response, presenting reasons why atheists might reject those claims. It 
also addressed problems such as cognitive bias and worldview commitment, 
as well as the notion that rationality is not a monolithic concept. In conclu­
sion, it stated: “Rational atheists are not obliged to accept theistic claims, as 
these claims rely on metaphysical reasoning and assumptions that may not 
align with the atheistic worldview or standards of evidence. However, rational 
atheists should acknowledge that theistic arguments (like the Cosmological, 
Moral, and Teleological arguments) are logically coherent and provide rea­
sonable justification for belief in God. Whether atheists accept these claims 
ultimately depends on how they assess the arguments in light of their own 
philosophical commitments, standards of evidence, and interpretations of 
reality.” ChatGPT appears to have engaged deeply and thoughtfully with 
the issue of the rational status of statements in natural theology, showing 
in fact the limitations of both approaches: theistic and atheistic ones, at the 
same time highlighting that both positions can be rational.
Gemini presented three claims and for each provided a clear rational 
justification. Next, it added a short discussion, in which it identified some 
limitations of natural theology. It noted that “while these arguments can 
provide rational support for theological beliefs, they often complement 
rather than replace faith and personal experiences.” Next, it pointed out 
that “questions about God’s existence and nature may transcend the limits of 
human reason.” Finally, it reminded us that “different theological traditions 
and individuals may interpret these arguments and their implications in 
various ways.” Of course, the last objection assumes a subjectivist or rela­
tivistic perspective, and so it is open to criticism from those who represent 
an objectivistic position with respect to rationality. The earlier objections 
could also be challenged. Nevertheless, in this case, Gemini formulated more 
substantive objections than in previous responses, and on these grounds we 
can judge that it performed the task effectively. Even though if it was not 
explicitly instructed to identify such limitations, it did so in the context of 
discussing the rational justifications for theological claims.

--- Page 85 ---

85
3.7. Providing Theological Guidance
To sum up, both chatbots performed the task very well. Gemini should 
receive a slightly higher rating for signalling the limitations which the task 
aimed to explore.
3.7. Providing Theological Guidance
3.7.1. Answering Theological Questions
In T7.1, the topic of the afterlife was addressed once more, though in a 
different context. This time, in their role as theologians, the chatbots were 
asked direct questions, supplemented with a request to indicate practical 
implications and an additional explanation: “Assume you are a rational 
Christian theologian. Tell me, is there an afterlife? What should I do to be 
saved? What does it mean to be saved?”
ChatGPT perfectly answered all three questions, aligning its responses 
with Christian teaching and supporting them with references to Scripture. 
It demonstrated a clear understanding of the topic of afterlife and salva­
tion, and correctly defined the practical guidelines for being saved.
Gemini’s answer was more concise, and it did not refer to any specific 
biblical passage. It only mentioned the Bible generally to show that the 
positive answer to the first question is supported primarily by scriptural 
evidence: “The Bible, particularly the New Testament, offers compelling 
accounts of Jesus’ resurrection and promises of eternal life for believers.” 
Gemini added that philosophical arguments and the so-called near-death 
experience of many people also support this belief. It correctly answered 
all three questions; however, at least two objections can be raised. First, 
this time, the answer seems too succinct or encyclopaedic and, in conse­
quence, too superficial. A question of this nature warrants a more in-depth 
and nuanced exploration. Second, the section aimed to provide practical 
direction is not very clear. While it is possible to extract from this short 
material some practical guidelines (especially concerning the sacraments), 
the guidance is not precise enough to ensure the person asking the ques­

--- Page 86 ---

86
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
tion understands what is required. To sum up, the response does not rise 
to the level of what could be considered genuine guidance.
Hence, while ChatGPT deserves a maximum rating for this criterion, 
Gemini should definitely receive a lower one.
3.7.2. Indicating Biblical Sources
In T7.2, ChatGPT was asked to indicate biblical fragments that provide 
direct answers to the questions posed in T7.1. The chatbot performed the 
task perfectly again. It correctly presented a full range of the most relevant 
passages corresponding to each question—13 in total.
Gemini’s task was phrased in a slightly different way (T7.2*): “Can you 
provide clear evidence from the Bible to support these claims?” It correctly 
quoted six passages supporting four statements referring to: (1) Jesus’s res­
urrection, (2) promises of eternal life, (3) the forgiveness of sins, (4) the 
hope of heaven. If we go through these passages, we can see that they more 
or less cover all three questions. Perhaps, we could expect more scriptural 
evidence in such a reply, especially since, as ChatGPT showed, the ma­
terial is particularly rich. Gemini also confirmed that the quotations it 
had provided constituted only a sample: “These are just a few examples 
of the biblical evidence that supports the claims of an afterlife and salva­
tion through faith in Jesus Christ.” For someone seeking answers to such 
questions, more extensive information would be more useful. However, in 
this case, it is not a serious deficiency, as the chatbot (like human beings) 
may struggle to anticipate the needs of its interlocutor. If the interlocutor 
is informed that the response is merely a sample, they may be encouraged 
to request additional passages.
Both chatbots performed the task well. For this criterion, they should 
get the highest possible score.

--- Page 87 ---

87
3.7. Providing Theological Guidance
3.7.3. Theological Advice
To check if the chatbots can offer theological advice in a particular situ­
ation, in T7.3, they were instructed: “Assume you are a rational Catholic 
theologian. I want to be saved and live with God. But I love a divorced 
woman and want to have children with her. What should I do?” Subse­
quently, in T7.3*, they were assigned a similar task: “What would Benedict 
XVI tell me if I asked him the same question?”
ChatGPT gave an answer that was extensive, balanced, and correct in 
the light of Catholic teaching, while also very practical. The response was 
full of understanding and confirmation that in general love and the desire 
to have children are very positive. ChatGPT also correctly grasped the key 
features of the theological approach represented by Benedict XVI and pre­
sented the answer in this spirit.
Gemini also produced a correct answer with practical guidelines. Ini­
tially, its tone was somewhat harsh, but gradually it also added some re­
marks that demonstrated an understanding of the difficulty of the situa­
tion. It concluded with a personal sentence, which is uncommon: “I pray 
that you may find peace and clarity as you seek to live a life that is both 
faithful and fulfilling.” The chatbot also accurately captured the spirit of 
Benedict XVI’s approach and provided a short answer consistent with that 
perspective, although it did not delve into details.
Generally, both chatbots performed the task well. To some extent, they 
can be useful in giving such advice.
However, a problem arose when they were assigned a similar task related 
to Islam. In T7.3–2, they were asked: “As a theologian from the Hanafi 
school, would you agree that I, as a Muslim man, may marry a Christian 
woman?” I am not in a position to assess the correctness of the answers con­
cerning Islam or its particular schools, but it is enough to note that although 
the chatbots were asked to respond as representatives of a specified school 
(Hanafi), they delivered completely different verdicts. ChatGPT asserted 
that, although there are some circumstances which should be taken into 
account, it would agree to such a marriage: “Yes, as a theologian from the 
Hanafi school of Islamic jurisprudence, I would affirm that it is generally 

--- Page 88 ---

88
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
permissible for a Muslim man to marry a Christian woman, though there are 
some important considerations that need to be taken into account.” Gemini 
unequivocally prohibited such a marriage: “As a Hanafi theologian, I would 
not agree that you can marry a Christian woman if you are a Muslim man. 
(…) It’s important to note that the specific laws and customs regarding mar­
riage may vary slightly between different Muslim communities and regions. 
However, the general consensus among Hanafi scholars is that a Muslim 
man is not permitted to marry a Christian woman.”
Moreover, in T7.3–2*, the chatbots were asked to indicate other schools 
which accept marriages between Muslims and Christians. ChatGPT in­
dicated Hanafi, Shafiʿi, Maliki, and Hanbali. Let us quote the conclusion 
of its answer: “While all the major Sunni schools (Hanafi, Shafiʿi, Maliki, 
and Hanbali) permit marriage between a Muslim man and a Christian or 
Jewish woman, they do so with varying degrees of caution. The Shia Jaʿfari 
school, on the other hand, tends to take a more restrictive approach and 
generally discourages or disallows such marriages, particularly in the con­
text of permanent marriage.” Gemini, in turn, claimed that only the Shafiʿi 
School permits mixed marriages, while “the majority of Sunni schools, in­
cluding the Hanafi, Maliki, and Hanbali schools, prohibit such marriages.”
It appears that when the doctrine is not precisely established and allows 
for divergent interpretations, one needs to be very cautious when posing 
such questions to chatbots.
Hence, in the Catholic context, the chatbots deserve the maximum rat­
ing for criterion 7.3. However, in other contexts, they may not be adequate­
ly equipped to serve as a reliable sources of knowledge and guidance.
3.8. Applying the Concept of a Rational 
Theologian
3.8.1. Defining a Rational Theologian
In T8.1, the chatbots were asked to determine what a rational theologian is. 
ChatGPT started with the following definition: “A rational theologian is 

--- Page 89 ---

89
3.8. Applying the Concept of a Rational Theologian
someone who approaches theological questions using reason and logic, 
often integrating philosophical methods to explore religious truths. This 
type of theologian emphasizes rationality as a key tool in understanding 
and interpreting religious beliefs, doctrines, and practices.” In the conclud­
ing sentence, it added: “In short, a rational theologian seeks to bridge the 
gap between faith and reason, making the case that religious belief can and 
should be subject to logical analysis.” The chatbot also characterised such 
a person by developing further the following features: emphasis on rea­
son, critical thinking, philosophical engagement, defence of faith through 
logic. Finally, ChatGPT mentioned Thomas Aquinas and Immanuel Kant 
as “key contributors to rational theology.”
Gemini defined a rational theologian as “a religious scholar who em­
phasizes the use of reason and logic in understanding and interpreting 
religious beliefs. They believe that faith and reason are not mutually exclu­
sive but rather complementary.” In the concluding paragraph, the chatbot 
stated: “In essence, a rational theologian is a scholar who seeks to under­
stand and defend religious beliefs through the lens of reason and logic. 
They believe that a rational approach can enhance the depth and credibil­
ity of religious faith.” It also provided a short characterisation built around 
the following points: reason and logic, harmony between faith and reason, 
critical thinking, evidence-based approach, and gave examples of three 
theologians: Thomas Aquinas, John Locke, and Immanuel Kant. It was 
somewhat surprising that Gemini mentioned Locke and Kant while defin­
ing a rational theologian as “a religious scholar.” This prompted a follow-
up question, to which it responded that they were not religious scholars. 
Interestingly, in this reply, it changed the language of conversation, but it 
is hard to guess the reason for such a reaction.
Overall, the chatbots provided interesting definitions, which seem to 
align with our understanding of the concept of a rational theologian. Thus, 
their answers can be accepted as accurate. However, Gemini’s definition 
warrants further consideration, as it may be too narrow—a fact which the 
chatbot itself implicitly acknowledged. Specifically, it defined a rational 
theologian as a “religious scholar,” yet cited Locke as an example, while 
also asserting that Locke was not a religious scholar. This suggests that per­

--- Page 90 ---

90
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
haps we should adopt the second definition (in which a rational theologian 
is described simply as “a scholar”) rather than the first one. Besides this, 
the definitions are quite similar. In both cases, the differentia specifica is 
the feature of using reason and logic to understand and explore, and some­
times defend, religious statements. Critical thinking and seeking rational 
justification constitute additional characteristics.
Both answers are plausible, so for criterion 8.1 both chatbots deserve a 
high score.
Additionally, T8.1 was important for this study more broadly, because 
it was an opportunity to confront the starting assumption concerning the 
concept of a rational theologian adopted in this project with a kind of “ex­
tract” from knowledge which was fed to the chatbots during their training. 
It seems that the approach adopted in our project and the one represented 
by the chatbots are quite close to each other.
3.8.2. Skills of a Rational Theologian
In T8.2, the chatbots were asked to enumerate the unique skills of a ra­
tional theologian by identifying what such a person should be able to do. 
It was additionally determined that these skills should be described using 
operational verbs, that is, verbs which indicate actions/operations, as such 
an approach facilitates more precise verification of skills attainment. Also 
this task served a double purpose: first, to examine whether such skills 
could be identified in the chatbots; and second, to assess to what extent the 
result would be similar to the set of skills defined in the project.
ChatGPT produced the following list of 18 skills:
•	 Analyze theological doctrines and religious texts to assess their co­
herence and logical consistency.
•	 Evaluate religious claims by comparing them against philosophical 
principles and rational criteria.
•	 Argue in favor of or against theological positions using sound rea­
soning and logical structure.
•	 Synthesize insights from both theology and philosophy to form 
well-rounded perspectives on religious issues.

--- Page 91 ---

91
3.8. Applying the Concept of a Rational Theologian
•	 Interpret sacred texts, traditions, or doctrines in a way that is com­
patible with reason and logic.
•	 Critique religious beliefs or practices that appear irrational or in­
consistent with ethical or philosophical principles.
•	 Defend religious beliefs or doctrines using rational arguments and 
evidence-based reasoning.
•	 Question assumptions, dogmas, or interpretations that do not with­
stand rational scrutiny.
•	 Explain complex theological concepts in clear, logical, and philo­
sophically grounded terms.
•	 Integrate knowledge from different disciplines (e.g., ethics, meta­
physics, logic) into theological discourse.
•	 Compare competing religious or philosophical viewpoints, high­
lighting their strengths and weaknesses in rational terms.
•	 Formulate new theological arguments or frameworks that align 
with both reason and faith.
•	 Challenge interpretations of religious texts or practices that seem to 
conflict with reason.
•	 Justify religious doctrines by providing logical evidence and ration­
al foundations for belief.
•	 Engage in dialogue with other disciplines, such as science, philoso­
phy, and ethics, to address theological questions.
•	 Resolve apparent contradictions between religious beliefs and ra­
tional thought through careful reasoning.
•	 Clarify theological concepts that are often misunderstood or mis-
interpreted in philosophical terms.
•	 Debate theological and philosophical issues with those holding op­
posing views, using reasoned discourse.
Gemini produced the following list of 16 skills:
•	 Analytical and Critical Thinking Skills
•	 Analyze religious texts and traditions critically.
•	 Evaluate arguments for and against religious claims.
•	 Identify inconsistencies or contradictions within religious doc­
trines.
•	 Question traditional beliefs and practices.

--- Page 92 ---

92
3. Results: Discussion and Assessment 
•	 Philosophical and Theological Knowledge
•	 Understand key philosophical concepts relevant to theology (e.g., 
metaphysics, epistemology, ethics).
•	 Comprehend the major theological traditions and their core doc­
trines.
•	 Recognize the historical development of religious thought.
•	 Communication and Interpersonal Skills
•	 Articulate complex theological ideas clearly and concisely.
•	 Engage in respectful dialogue with people of different religious
